2.
Pseudolattices

2.1.
Background

We begin by briefly recapping some relevant background from the theory of pseudolattices, based on
[
Kuz17
,
HT20
]
; a much more detailed treatment of these concepts may be found in those two papers. We start with some basic definitions.

Definition 2.1
.

[
Kuz17
, Definition 2.1]

A
pseudolattice

(\mathrm{G},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}})
is a finitely generated free abelian group
\mathrm{G}
equipped with a (not necessarily symmetric) nondegenerate bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\colon\mathrm{G}\times\mathrm{G}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
.
The
rank
of
\mathrm{G}
is its rank as a free abelian group and
\mathrm{G}
is called
unimodular
if the form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathrm{G},\mathbb{Z})
. To simplify notation, we will usually refer to
(\mathrm{G},\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}})
by its underlying group
\mathrm{G}
.

A simple example of a pseudolattice, that we will use repeatedly, is the
elliptic curve pseudolattice
.

Definition 2.2
.

[
HT20
, Definition 2.14]
The
elliptic curve pseudolattice
, henceforth denoted by
\mathrm{E}
, is the unimodular pseudolattice of rank
2
generated by primitive elements
a,b
, with bilinear form defined by

\langle a,b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=-1,\quad\quad\langle b,a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=1,\quad\quad\langle a,a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=\langle b,b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0,

extended to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by linearity.

The pseudolattice
\mathrm{E}
arises naturally from an elliptic curve
C
in two ways (see
[
HT20
, Example 2.15]
).

(1)

The numerical Grothendieck group
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
associated to the bounded derived category
\mathbf{D}(C)
of coherent sheaves on
C
, with its Euler pairing, is isomorphic to
\mathrm{E}
. The element
a
is identified with the class of the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{p}
of a point
p\in C
, and
b
may be identified with the class of
\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

(2)

The first integral homology
\mathrm{H}_{1}(C;\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{E}
. In this case
(a,b)
is taken to be a symplectic basis for
\mathrm{H}_{1}(C;\mathbb{Z})
and the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}
is the negative of the standard symplectic form
1
1

1

[
HT20
, Example 2.15]
does not specify the orientation of the basis
(a,b)
or the sign convention for the bilinear form; this is the correct choice for compatibility with the results of
[
HT20
, Section 4]
.
. This may also be interpreted as the numerical Grothendieck group associated to a triangulated category, in this case the
derived Fukaya category

\mathbf{F}(C)
associated to
C
.

These two interpretations can be thought of as mirror to one another
[
HT20
, Section 5]
; the first will be important for us in Section
3
and the second in Section
4
.

We now define some important structures on pseudolattices:
Serre operators
and
exceptional bases
.

Definition 2.3
.

[
Kuz17
, Section 2.1]

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a pseudolattice. A
Serre operator

S_{\mathrm{G}}
is an automorphism of
\mathrm{G}
so that
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle u_{2},S_{\mathrm{G}}(u_{1})\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
for all
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathrm{G}
.

We note that if a Serre operator exists then it is unique, and that every unimodular pseudolattice has a Serre operator.

Definition 2.4
.

[
Kuz17
, Definition 2.2]

An element
e\in\mathrm{G}
is called
exceptional
if
\langle e,e\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=1
. A sequence of elements
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
is called an
exceptional sequence
if
\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0
for all
i>j
. An
exceptional basis
for
\mathrm{G}
is an exceptional sequence whose elements form a basis for
\mathrm{G}
.

A subclass of pseudolattices, that will be important for this paper, is the
surface-like
pseudolattices.

Definition 2.5
.

[
Kuz17
, Definition 3.1]

A pseudolattice
\mathrm{G}
is
surface-like
if there is a primitive element
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{G}
such that

(1)

\langle\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0
.

(2)

\langle\mathbf{p},u\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle u,\mathbf{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
for all
u\in\mathrm{G}
.

(3)

If
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathrm{G}
satisfy
\langle u_{1},\mathbf{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle u_{2},\mathbf{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0
, then
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle u_{2},u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
.

An element
\mathbf{p}
with the above properties is called a
point-like
element in
\mathrm{G}
.

Using a point-like element, one may define the
Néron-Severi lattice
of a surface-like pseudolattice
\rm{G}
.

Definition 2.6
.

[
Kuz17
, Section 3.2]

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a surface-like pseudolattice. Define the
Néron-Severi group
of
\mathrm{G}
to be the group
\NS(\mathrm{G}):={\mathbf{p}^{\perp}}/\mathbf{p}
. It is a finitely generated free abelian group, equipped with a nondegenerate integral symmetric bilinear form
q(\cdot,\cdot)
, defined as follows: if
u_{1},u_{2}\in{\mathbf{p}}^{\perp}
and
[u_{1}],[u_{2}]
are their classes in
\NS(\mathrm{G})
, then
q\left([u_{1}],[u_{2}]\right):=-\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
, and this definition is independent of the choice of representatives for
[u_{1}]
,
[u_{2}]
. The pair
(\NS(\mathrm{G}),q(\cdot,\cdot))
is called the
Néron-Severi lattice
of
\mathrm{G}
. As before, to simplify notation, we will usually refer to
(\NS(\mathrm{G}),q(\cdot,\cdot))
by its underlying group
\NS(\mathrm{G})
.

Following
[
Kuz17
, Section 3.2]
, one may define a rank function on a surface-like pseudolattice
\mathrm{G}
as follows. For
u\in\mathrm{G}
, we have
\rank(u):=\langle u,\mathbf{p}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle\mathbf{p},u\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
.
Using this, one may define a map
\lambda\colon\bigwedge^{2}\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{NS}(\mathrm{G})
by

\lambda(u_{1}\wedge u_{2}):=[\rank(u_{1})u_{2}-\rank(u_{2})u_{1}].

This map can be used to define a distinguished class
K_{\mathrm{G}}
in
\NS(\mathrm{G})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
, called the
canonical class
of
\mathrm{G}
. Existence and uniqueness of the canonical class is established in
[
Kuz17
, Lemma 3.12]
.

Definition 2.7
.

The
canonical class of
\mathrm{G}
is the unique class
K_{\mathrm{G}}\in\mathrm{NS}(\mathrm{G})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
such that for every
u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathrm{G}
,

\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}-\langle u_{2},u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=-q(K_{\mathrm{G}},\lambda(u_{1}\wedge u_{2})).

By
[
Kuz17
, Lemma 3.12]
, if
\mathrm{G}
is unimodular, then
K_{\mathrm{G}}
is integral (i.e.
K_{\mathrm{G}}\in\mathrm{NS}(\mathrm{G})
).

We next introduce a special class of maps between pseudolattices.

Definition 2.8
.

[
HT20
, Definition 2.16]

Let
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{H}
be pseudolattices. A
spherical homomorphism
from
\mathrm{G}
to
\mathrm{H}
is a homomorphism of abelian groups
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{H}
with the following properties:

(1)

The homomorphism
f
has a right adjoint
r\colon\mathrm{H}\to\mathrm{G}
, in the sense that

\langle f(u),v\rangle_{\mathrm{H}}=\langle u,r(v)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

for any
u\in\mathrm{G}
and
v\in\mathrm{H}
.

(2)

The
twist
and
cotwist
endomorphisms, which are defined as

{T}_{f}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{H}}-fr,\quad\quad{C}_{f}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{G}}-rf,

respectively, are invertible. In fact, if
{T}_{f}
is invertible with inverse
T_{f}^{-1}
, then
{C}_{f}
is invertible with inverse
C_{f}^{-1}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{G}}+rT_{f}^{-1}f
, and vice versa.

Note that, by
[
HT20
, Remark 2.17]
, if a right adjoint for a homomorphism
f
exists, then it must be unique, and a right adjoint always exists if
\mathrm{G}
is unimodular.

Definition 2.9
.

[
HT20
, Definition 2.18]

A spherical homomorphism
f\colon\mathrm{G}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}
is
relative
(-1)^{n}
-Calabi-Yau
(usually abbreviated to
relative
(-1)^{n}
-CY
) if
\mathrm{G}
has a Serre operator
S_{\mathrm{G}}
and
{C}_{f}=(-1)^{n}S_{\mathrm{G}}
.

Note that this definition only depends upon the parity of
n
; consequently we will usually take
n
to be an element of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. An example of a relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphism that will be important for this paper is as follows.

Example 2.10
.

[
HT20
, Example 2.20]

Let
\mathrm{E}
be the elliptic curve pseudolattice and let
v\in\mathrm{E}
be a primitive vector. Note that
\langle v,v\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
, as the bilinear form on
\mathrm{E}
is antisymmetric. Let
\mathrm{Z}(v)
be the unimodular pseudolattice generated by a single element
z
, with
\langle z,z\rangle_{\mathrm{Z}(v)}=1
. Note that the Serre operator on
\mathrm{Z}(v)
is the identity and
(z)
is an exceptional basis for
\mathrm{Z}(v)
.

There is a spherical homomorphism
\zeta\colon\mathrm{Z}(v)\rightarrow\mathrm{E}
sending
z
to
v
. The right adjoint of this homomorphism is
\rho\colon\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{Z}(v)
defined by
w\mapsto\langle v,w\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}z
.
The cotwist of
\zeta
is the identity on
\mathbb{Z}(v)
, so it follows that
\zeta
is relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY. The twist of
\zeta
acts on
w\in\mathrm{E}
by
T_{\zeta}(w)=w-\langle v,w\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}v
. If we identify
\mathrm{E}
with
\mathrm{H}_{1}(C;\mathbb{Z})
for an elliptic curve
C
, and
v\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(C;\mathbb{Z})
is the class of a simple oriented cycle, then
T_{\zeta}
is the action on
\mathrm{H}_{1}(C;\mathbb{Z})
induced by the Dehn twist associated to
v
.

We can use spherical homomorphisms to glue pseudolattices together.

Definition 2.11
.

[
HT20
, Definition 2.22]

Let
\mathrm{G}_{1},\mathrm{G}_{2}
and
\mathrm{H}
be pseudolattices. Assume that

f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\longrightarrow\mathrm{H},\quad\quad f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{H}

are homomorphisms of the underlying groups. Define
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
to be the pseudolattice with underlying group
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{G}_{2}
and bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{G}_{2}}
given as follows: for
u_{i}\in\mathrm{G}_{i}
and
v_{j}\in\mathrm{G}_{j}
, define

\langle u_{i},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{G}_{2}}=\begin{cases}\langle u_{i},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}&\text{if }i=j\\
\langle f_{i}(u_{i}),f_{j}(v_{j})\rangle_{\mathrm{H}}&\text{if }i=1,\ j=2\\
0&\text{if }i=2,\ j=1\end{cases}

and extend to
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{G}_{2}
by linearity. By
[
HT20
, Proposition 2.23]
, there is a natural spherical homomorphism
f_{1}\oright f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{G}_{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}
, sending
(u_{1},u_{2})\in\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{H}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
to
f_{1}(u_{1})+f_{2}(u_{2})
, which has the property that
{T}_{f_{1}\oright f_{2}}={T}_{f_{1}}\cdot{T}_{f_{2}}
.

We will mostly be interested in the case of spherical homomorphisms where the target is the elliptic curve pseudolattice
\mathrm{E}
. In this case we have the following result, which is a special case of
[
HT20
, Proposition 2.23]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Given two pseudolattices
\mathrm{G}_{1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
with relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphisms
f_{i}\colon\mathrm{G}_{i}\rightarrow\mathrm{E}
, then
f_{1}\oright f_{2}
is also relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY.

As an important example of this gluing process, we can glue the spherical homomorphisms
\mathrm{Z}(v)\to\mathrm{E}
from Example
2.10
to build pseudolattices of higher rank.

Example 2.13
.

[
HT20
, Example 2.24]

Suppose we have an ordered
n
-tuple of elements
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
in
\mathrm{E}
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, define rank one pseudolattices
\mathrm{Z}(v_{i})
generated by elements
z_{i}
with
\langle z_{i},z_{i}\rangle_{\mathrm{Z}(v_{i})}=1
, as in Example
2.10
. By Example
2.10
, we have relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphisms
\zeta_{i}\colon\mathrm{Z}(v_{i})\to\mathrm{E}
taking
z_{i}
to
v_{i}
.

Let
\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
denote the pseudolattice

\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}):=\mathrm{Z}(v_{1})\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{Z}(v_{2})\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\cdots\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{Z}(v_{n}).

Then
\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
is a unimodular pseudolattice of rank
n
generated by
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}
, with bilinear form given by

\langle z_{i},z_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})}=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }i=j\\
\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}&\text{ if }i<j\\
0&\text{ if }i>j.\end{cases}

(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
thus form an exceptional basis for
Z(v_{1},v_{2})
.

The spherical homomorphism
\zeta=\zeta_{1}\oright\cdots\oright\zeta_{n}\colon\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{E}
sending
z_{i}
to
v_{i}
is relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY and the twist
{T}_{\zeta}={T}_{\zeta_{1}}\cdots{T}_{\zeta_{n}}
.

Spherical homomorphisms to the elliptic curve pseudolattice give a convenient way to construct surface-like pseudolattices. The following result is a combination of the results of
[
HT20
, Propositions 3.1, 3.2, 3.3]
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Suppose that
(a,b)
is a basis for
\mathrm{E}
as in Definition
2.2
. Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
be a relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphism with right adjoint
r
. Then
\mathrm{G}
is surface-like with point-like vector
\mathbf{p}=r(a)
if and only if
r(a)
is primitive and the twist
T_{f}
satisfies
T_{f}(a)=a
. Moreover, if this holds, then:

(1)

r(b)\in\mathbf{p}^{\perp}
and
[r(b)]=-K_{\mathrm{G}}
, where the square brackets
[-]
denote the class in
\NS(\mathrm{G})
.

(2)

In the basis
(a,b)
, the twist
T_{f}
has matrix
\begin{pmatrix}1&-d\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, where
d:=q(K_{\mathrm{G}},K_{\mathrm{G}})
.

Now we come to the central definition of
[
HT20
]
.

Definition 2.15
.

[
HT20
, Definition 3.7]

Let
\mathrm{G}
be a pseudolattice of rank
n
. A spherical homomorphism of pseudolattices
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
is called a
quasi del Pezzo homomorphism
if there exists a basis
(a,b)
for
\mathrm{E}
, as in Definition
2.2
, so that all of the following conditions hold.

(1)

\mathrm{G}
is surface-like with point-like element
\mathbf{p}=r(a)
.

(2)

f
is relative
(-1)^{0}
-Calabi-Yau.

(3)

\mathrm{G}
admits an exceptional basis
e_{\bullet}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})
such that
f(e_{i})\in\mathrm{E}
is primitive for each
1\leq i\leq n
.

(4)

\NS(\mathrm{G})
has signature
(1,n-3)
.

Note that conditions (1) and (2) from this definition imply that Proposition
2.14
holds for all quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms. The motivating example behind this definition is as follows.

Example 2.16
.

[
HT20
, Example 3.10]
Let
V
be a nonsingular rational surface and suppose that the anticanonical linear system
|-K_{V}|
contains a smooth member
C
; such surfaces are called
quasi del Pezzo surfaces
. Note that, by adjunction,
C
is an elliptic curve. Let
i^{*}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\to\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
be the map between the numerical Groethendieck groups of the bounded derived categories of coherent sheaves
\mathbf{D}(V)
and
\mathbf{D}(C)
on
V
and
C
, respectively, given by the derived pull-back under the inclusion
i\colon C\hookrightarrow V
. Then
i^{*}
is a relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphism, with right adjoint
i_{*}
given by derived push-forward.

Identify
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))\cong\mathrm{E}
as above: identify
a
with the class
[\mathcal{O}_{p}]
of the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{p}
of a point
p\in C
and
b
with the class
[\mathcal{O}_{C}]
of
\mathcal{O}_{C}
. Then
i^{*}
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism of pseudolattices.

The point-like element
\mathbf{p}=i_{*}([\mathcal{O}_{p}])
is the class of the structure sheaf of a point in
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))
. With respect to this point-like element we have an isomorphism of lattices

\mathbf{p}^{\perp}/\mathbf{p}=:\NS(\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V)))\cong\NS(V)\cong H^{2}(V;\mathbb{Z}),

which takes the canonical class in
\NS(\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V)))
(Definition
2.7
) to the usual canonical class in
\NS(V)
.

The main result of
[
HT20
]
is a classification of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms. In order to state it, we first need to say what it means for two quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms to be isomorphic.

Definition 2.17
.

[
HT20
, Definition 3.9]
Two quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
are
isomorphic
if

•

there are bases
(a_{1},b_{1})
and
(a_{2},b_{2})
for
\mathrm{E}
, so that
(a_{i},b_{i})
satisfies the conditions of Definition
2.15
for
f_{i}\colon\mathrm{G}_{i}\to\mathrm{E}
, and an automorphism
\varphi\colon\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{E}
taking
(a_{1},b_{1})
to
(a_{2},b_{2})
,

•

there is an isomorphism of pseudolattices
\psi\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{G}_{2}
(i.e. an isomorphism of the underlying abelian groups which preserves the bilinear form), and

•

we have
\varphi f_{1}=f_{2}\psi
.

It is easy to check that the last part of this definition implies that
\psi r_{1}=r_{2}\varphi
, where
r_{i}
is the right adjoint to
f_{i}
.

The next theorem is the main result of
[
HT20
]
, which classifies quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms up to isomorphism. The version stated here is a slight simplification of the original.

Theorem 2.18
.

[
HT20
, Theorem 3.25]
Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
be a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism and let
n:=\rank(\mathrm{G})
. Then
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
is isomorphic to exactly one of:

(1)

\mathrm{Z}(b,3a+b,6a+b,a,\ldots,a)\to\mathrm{E}
, for any
n\geq 3
; or

(2)

\mathrm{Z}(b,2a+b,2a+b,4a+b)\to\mathrm{E}
for
n=4
.

It is not difficult to show (c.f.
[
Dem80
]
,
[
CT88
, Proposition 0.4]
) that if
V
is a quasi del Pezzo surface and
C\subset V
is a smooth anticanonical divisor, then
(V,C)
is either

•

a blow-up of a smooth cubic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
k\geq 0
points in almost general position (a set of points is in
almost general position
if no stage of the blowing-up involves blowing up a point which lies on a rational
(-2)
-curve; infinitely near points are allowed as long as this condition is not violated), or

•

a smooth curve of bidegree
(2,2)
in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, or

•

a smooth anticanonical curve in the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

It then follows from Theorem
2.18
,
[
HT20
, Example 3.10]
, and the analysis in
[
HT20
, Section 3.3]
, that every quasi del Pezzo homomorphism is isomorphic to
i^{*}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\to\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
for
V
a quasi del Pezzo surface and
i\colon C\hookrightarrow V
a smooth anticanonical divisor. Explicitly, case (1) of Theorem
2.18
corresponds to the case where
V
is a blow-up of a smooth cubic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
n-3
points in almost general position, and case (2) of Theorem
2.18
corresponds to the case where
V\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
V\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}
.

The main observation of
[
HT20
]
is that quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms also arise naturally from genus
1
Lefschetz fibrations over closed discs
Y\to\Delta
. In this setting they occur as boundary maps
H_{2}(Y,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
, where
F
is a smooth fibre over a point on the boundary of
\Delta
and we place pseudolattice structures on
H_{2}(Y,F;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
via the
Seifert pairing
and the usual intersection form respectively; we refer the reader to Section
4.1
for a more detailed treatment.

The fact that quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms arise in both of these settings should be thought of as a manifestation of Fano-LG mirror symmetry for quasi del Pezzo surfaces; see
[
HT20
, Section 5]
.

2.2.
Further properties of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms

In this subsection we will collect some additional properties of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms, which do not come from
[
Kuz17
,
HT20
]
, that will be useful to us in the rest of the paper.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
be a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism of pseudolattices and let
r\colon\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{G}
denote the right adjoint of
f
. Let
(a,b)
be a basis for
\mathrm{E}
as in Definition
2.15
. Then
r
is injective and satisfies
fr(a)=0
.

Proof.

Injectivity of
r
is an easy consequence of the explicit classification of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms (Theorem
2.18
). Moreover, as
f
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism, Proposition
2.14
gives
T_{f}(a)=a
. But
T_{f}(a)=a-fr(a)
by definition, so we must have
fr(a)=0
.
∎

We next collect some information about the Néron-Severi lattice associated to a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
be a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism and let
n:=\rank(\mathrm{G})
.

(1)

If
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
is isomorphic to case (1) in Theorem
2.18
, then
\NS(\mathrm{G})
is the odd unimodular lattice
\mathrm{I}_{1,n-3}
of signature
(1,n-3)
. The canonical class
K_{\mathrm{G}}
has
q(K_{\mathrm{G}},K_{\mathrm{G}})=12-n
and its orthogonal complement
K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}
in
\NS(\mathrm{G})
is given in Table
2.1
. This orthogonal complement is negative definite if
n<12
, degenerate if
n=12
, and indefinite of signature
(1,n-4)
if
n>12
. If
n\notin\{3,12\}
, the discriminant group
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{Z}\,/\,|12-n|\mathbb{Z}
. The quadratic form on
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp})
is generated by an element
e
with
e^{2}=\frac{1}{n-12}
, with values taken modulo
\mathbb{Z}
if
n
is odd and modulo
2\mathbb{Z}
if
n
is even.

(2)

If
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
is isomorphic to case (2) in Theorem
2.18
, then
\NS(\mathrm{G})
is the even unimodular lattice
H=\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}_{1,1}
of signature
(1,1)
(the hyperbolic plane). The canonical class
K_{\mathrm{G}}
has
q(K_{\mathrm{G}},K_{\mathrm{G}})=8
and the orthogonal complement
K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}
is the root lattice
A_{1}
, which is negative definite with
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. The quadratic form on
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp})
is generated by an element
e
with
e^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}
, with values taken modulo
2\mathbb{Z}
.

n

3

4

5

6

7

8

\geq 9

K_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}

\{0\}

(-8)

\begin{pmatrix}-2&1\\
1&-4\end{pmatrix}

A_{2}\oplus A_{1}

A_{4}

D_{5}

E_{n-3}

Table 2.1
.
Néron-Severi lattices in case (1) of Lemma
2.20
. Here
A_{n}
,
D_{n}
,
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, and
E_{8}
are the usual negative definite root lattices, and we extend the definition of the
E_{n}
series of lattices to
n\geq 9
in the obvious way.

Proof.

This follows from the isomorphism between
\NS(\mathrm{G})
and
\NS(V)
for a quasi del Pezzo surface
V
, along with the well-known lattice theory for quasi del Pezzo surfaces (see, for example,
[
Dol12
, Chapter 8]
).
∎

Now we come to the main result of this section, which characterises isomorphisms between quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms in terms of their action on the Néron-Severi lattices.

Proposition 2.21
.

Suppose that
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
are two quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms.

If
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
are isomorphic, in the sense of Definition
2.17
, then the isomorphism
\psi\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{G}_{2}
induces an isometry
\hat{\psi}\colon\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\to\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
such that
\hat{\psi}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
.

Conversely, if
\hat{\psi}\colon\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\to\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
is an isometry such that
\hat{\psi}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
, then there exists an isomorphism between
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
that induces
\hat{\psi}
.

Proof.

Let
r_{i}
denote the right adjoint to
f_{i}
. Suppose first that there exists an isomorphism between
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
, in the sense of Definition
2.17
. Then there are bases
(a_{1},b_{1})
and
(a_{2},b_{2})
for
\mathrm{E}
so that
(a_{i},b_{i})
satisfies the conditions of Definition
2.15
for
f_{i}\colon\mathrm{G}_{i}\to\mathrm{E}
, and an automorphism
\varphi\colon\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{E}
taking
(a_{1},b_{1})
to
(a_{2},b_{2})
, along with an isomorphism
\psi\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{G}_{2}
which commutes with
\varphi
.

As
\psi r_{1}=r_{2}\varphi
, we have
\psi(r_{1}(a_{1}))=r_{2}(\varphi(a_{1}))=r_{2}(a_{2})
, so
\psi
maps the point-like vector in
\mathrm{G}_{1}
to the point-like vector in
\mathrm{G}_{2}
. It follows that
\psi
induces an isometry
\hat{\psi}\colon\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\to\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
. Moreover, as
\psi(r_{1}(b_{1}))=r_{2}(\varphi(b_{1}))=r_{2}(b_{2})
and
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=-[r_{i}(b_{i})]
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\hat{\psi}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
.

Next, suppose that
\hat{\psi}\colon\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\to\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
is an isometry such that
\hat{\psi}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
.

By the classification of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms, the homomorphism
f_{j}\colon\mathrm{G}_{j}\to\mathrm{E}
is isomorphic to
i^{*}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))\to\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C_{j}))
for
V_{j}
a quasi del Pezzo surface and
i\colon C_{j}\hookrightarrow V_{j}
a smooth anticanonical divisor. We may identify
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C_{j}))\cong\mathrm{E}
in the usual way: identify
a_{j}
with the class of a point
p\in C_{j}
and
b_{j}
with the class of
\mathcal{O}_{C_{j}}
. Moreover, by
[
Kuz17
, Example 3.5]
, there are isomorphisms
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\NS(V_{j})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
given by
v\mapsto(\rank(v),c_{1}(v),\chi(v))
, i.e. the rank, first Chern class, and Euler characteristic of an element
v\in\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))
.

Choose bases for
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{j})
as free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules for each
j\in\{1,2\}
. Let
Q_{j}
denote the matrix of the intersection form and let
K_{j}
denote the column vector representing
K_{\mathrm{G}_{j}}
with respect to this basis. Then in the realisation
\mathrm{G}_{j}\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\NS(V_{j})\oplus\mathbb{Z}
given above, one may compute that the bilinear form on
\mathrm{G}_{j}
is given explicitly by

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&1\\
Q_{j}K_{j}&-Q_{j}&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}

and the map
f_{j}\colon\mathrm{G}_{j}\to\mathrm{E}
is given explicitly by

\begin{pmatrix}0&-K_{j}^{T}Q_{j}&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}

with respect to the basis
a_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}
and
b_{j}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}
of
\mathrm{E}
.

Let
P
be the matrix of the isometry
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\to\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
with respect to the chosen bases of these two lattices. Then
P^{T}Q_{2}P=Q_{1}
and
PK_{1}=K_{2}
. Define a map
\psi\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{G}_{2}
by the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&P&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

and let
\varphi
be the automorphism
\varphi\colon\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{E}
taking
(a_{1},b_{1})
to
(a_{2},b_{2})
. Then it is a straightforward exercise in linear algebra to show that
\psi
defines an isomorphism of pseudolattices
\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{G}_{2}
and that
\varphi f_{1}=f_{2}\psi
, so we obtain an isomorphism of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms between
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
and
f_{2}\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
.
∎

2.3.
From pseudolattices to lattices

With this background in place, we will explore what happens when one glues pairs of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms. In particular, we will show that if this is done in the correct way, it gives rise to an exact sequence of lattices.

Let
f_{i}\colon\mathrm{G}_{i}\to\mathrm{E}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
be two quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms of pseudolattices, as in Definition
2.15
, and suppose further that the basis
(a,b)
for
\mathrm{E}
appearing in this definition can be taken to be the same for both
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
r_{i}
denote the right adjoint of
f_{i}
. Finally, assume that
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=-q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})
.

The homomorphism
(-f_{2})\colon\mathrm{G}_{2}\to\mathrm{E}
is clearly spherical, with right adjoint
(-r_{2})
and twist and cotwist
T_{(-f_{2})}=T_{f_{2}}
and
C_{(-f_{2})}=C_{f_{2}}
respectively. From this it follows immediately that
(-f_{2})
is also relatively
(-1)^{0}
-CY.

Now define
\mathrm{G}:=\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
glued along the homomorphisms
f_{1}
and
(-f_{2})
, and let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
be the natural homomorphism

\displaystyle f\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}\longrightarrow\mathrm{E}.

\displaystyle f(u_{1},u_{2})=f_{1}(u_{1})-f_{2}(u_{2}).

By Lemma
2.12
,
f
is a relative
(-1)^{0}
-CY spherical homomorphism. We denote the right adjoint of
f
by
r
. By the proof of
[
HT20
, Proposition 2.23]
, the map
r
is given explicitly by

(1)

r(v)=(r_{1}T_{f_{2}}(v),-r_{2}(v))\in\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}

for any
v\in\mathrm{E}
. Let
\mathrm{K}\subset\mathrm{G}
denote the kernel of
f
.

Lemma 2.22
.

The bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
is symmetric on
\mathrm{K}
, so
\mathrm{K}
is a lattice
(
in the classical sense
)
.

Proof.

Let
S_{\mathrm{G}}
denote the Serre operator of
\mathrm{G}
; as
f
is relatively
(-1)^{0}
-CY, we have
S_{\mathrm{G}}=C_{f}:=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{G}}-rf
. Then for any
u,v\in\mathrm{K}
, we have

\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,S_{\mathrm{G}}(u)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,u-rf(u)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,u\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

as
f(u)=0
by assumption.
∎

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
u\in\mathrm{E}
. Then
r(u)\in\mathrm{K}
and
\langle v,r(u)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0
for all
v\in\mathrm{K}
, so
\im(r)
is a totally degenerate sublattice of
\mathrm{K}
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.11
, the twist
T_{f}=T_{f_{1}}\cdot T_{-f_{2}}=T_{f_{1}}\cdot T_{f_{2}}
. Moreover, by Proposition
2.14
, in the basis
(a,b)
for
\mathrm{E}
we have
T_{f_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-d_{i}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
, where
d_{i}:=q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}})
. Thus, since
d_{1}=-d_{2}
by assumption, we have
T_{f}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{E}}
. But
T_{f}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{E}}-fr
by definition, so
fr(u)=0
for all
u\in\mathrm{E}
and hence
r(u)\in\mathrm{K}
.

To prove the second part of the statement, note that by adjunction
\langle v,r(u)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle f(v),u\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
,
as
f(v)=0
by assumption.
∎

Let
\overline{\mathrm{E}}\subset\mathrm{K}
denote the saturation of the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule
\im(r)
, equipped with the bilinear form induced by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
; by the results above we see that
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the zero form. By Lemma
2.23
, we may take the quotient
\mathrm{K}/\overline{\mathrm{E}}
to obtain a new lattice, which we denote by
\mathrm{M}
. We thus have an exact sequence of lattices (in the classical sense)

(2)

0\longrightarrow\overline{\mathrm{E}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}\longrightarrow\mathrm{M}\longrightarrow 0.

Remark 2.24
.

Note that if
(u_{1},u_{2})
and
(v_{1},v_{2})
are elements of
\mathrm{K}
, for
u_{i},v_{i}\in\mathrm{G}_{i}
, then as
\mathrm{K}=\ker(f)
we have
f_{1}(u_{1})=f_{2}(u_{2})
and
f_{1}(v_{1})=f_{2}(v_{2})
. Consequently

\displaystyle\langle(u_{1},u_{2}),\,(v_{1},v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle u_{1},v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}-\langle f_{1}(u_{1}),f_{2}(v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}+\langle u_{2},v_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle u_{2},v_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}-\langle f_{2}(u_{2}),f_{1}(v_{1})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}+\langle u_{1},v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\langle(u_{2},u_{1}),\,(v_{2},v_{1})\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{2}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{1}}

It follows that switching the order of the factors in the definition of
\mathrm{G}
does not change the lattice
\mathrm{K}
: we obtain the same
\mathrm{K}
if we define
\mathrm{G}
to be
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
or
\mathrm{G}_{2}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{1}
. From this it is easy to see that the remaining results of this section, particularly the exact sequence (
2
), are also independent of the ordering of the factors in
\mathrm{G}
.

As a consequence of this remark, we may freely switch the labels on
\mathrm{G}_{1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
without affecting the subsequent development. For concreteness, we will henceforth assume that labels have been chosen so that
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})\geq 0
.

Definition 2.25
.

Define the
degree
of
\mathrm{G}
to be
\deg(\mathrm{G})=q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})
, whenever
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})\neq 8
. If
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=8
then, by Theorem
2.18
and Lemma
2.20
, there are two possibilities for the quasi del Pezzo homomorphism
f_{1}\colon\mathrm{G}_{1}\to\mathrm{E}
: we say that
\deg(\mathrm{G})=8
if
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\cong\mathrm{I}_{1,1}
(case (1) of Theorem
2.18
) and
\deg(\mathrm{G})=8^{\prime}
if
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\cong\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}_{1,1}
(case (2) of Theorem
2.18
).

As a consequence of the classification of quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms (Theorem
2.18
), we note that
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
is determined up to isomorphism by
\deg(\mathrm{G})
, and that
0\leq\deg(\mathrm{G})\leq 9
.

2.4.
Point-like vectors and the Néron-Severi lattice

Next we consider the vectors
(r_{1}(a),0)
and
(0,r_{2}(a))
in
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}=\mathrm{G}
. Define
\Psi
to be the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule of
\mathrm{G}
generated by these two elements.

Lemma 2.26
.

\Psi
has the following properties.

(1)

\Psi
is primitive in
\mathrm{G}
.

(2)

If
p\in\Psi
, then
\langle p,v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,p\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
for any
v\in\mathrm{G}
.

(3)

\Psi
is contained in
\mathrm{K}
.

(4)

The restriction of the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
to
\Psi
is identically zero.

(5)

\Psi\cap\overline{\mathrm{E}}
is generated by the primitive element

r(a)=(r_{1}(a),-r_{2}(a))\in\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}=\mathrm{G}.

Proof.

To prove (1), note that
r_{i}(a)
is primitive in
\mathrm{G}_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, by Proposition
2.14
. As
\mathrm{G}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oplus\mathrm{G}_{2}
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, it follows that
\Psi
is primitive in
\mathrm{G}
.

To prove (2), it suffices to show that
\langle r_{i}(a),v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,r_{i}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and any
v\in\mathrm{G}
. So let
v\in\mathrm{G}
. Writing
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
for
v_{i}\in\mathrm{G}_{i}
, by Definition
2.11
we have

\displaystyle\langle r_{1}(a),v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle r_{1}(a),v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}+\langle f_{1}r_{1}(a),-f_{2}(v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}},

\displaystyle\langle v,r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{1},r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}.

But
f_{1}r_{1}(a)=0
by Lemma
2.19
, so
\langle r_{1}(a),v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle r_{1}(a),v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}
.
To complete the proof, we use the fact that, by Definition
2.15
,
r_{1}(a)
is point-like in
\mathrm{G}_{1}
, so
\langle r_{1}(a),v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}=\langle v_{1},r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}
;
we conclude that
\langle r_{1}(a),v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle v,r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
as required. The proof for
r_{2}
is completely analogous.

To prove (3), we have
f(r_{1}(a),0)=f_{1}r_{1}(a)
and
f(0,r_{2}(a))=-f_{2}r_{2}(a)
, and both of these are equal to
0
by Lemma
2.19
. So
(r_{1}(a),0),(0,r_{2}(a))\in\ker(f)=\mathrm{K}
, which implies
\Psi\subset\mathrm{K}
.

To prove (4) we note that, as
r_{i}(a)
is point-like in
\mathrm{G}_{i}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, we must have
\langle r_{i}(a),r_{i}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=0
. So, by Definition
2.11
,

\langle(r_{1}(a),0),(r_{1}(a),0)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle(0,r_{2}(a)),(0,r_{2}(a))\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0

Moreover,
\langle(0,r_{2}(a)),(r_{1}(a),0)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=0
by definition and

\langle(r_{1}(a),0),(0,r_{2}(a))\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle f_{1}r_{1}(a),-f_{2}r_{2}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0,

as
f_{i}r_{i}(a)=0
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
by Lemma
2.19
. We conclude that the restriction of the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}
to
\Psi
is identically zero.

Finally, to prove (5) we observe that, by Equation (
1
), the map
r
is given by

r(v)=(r_{1}T_{f_{2}}(v),-r_{2}(v))=(r_{1}(v)-r_{1}f_{2}r_{2}(v),-r_{2}(v)).

Applying this to
a\in\mathrm{E}
, we see that
r(a)=(r_{1}(a),-r_{2}(a))
, where we have used the fact that
f_{2}r_{2}(a)=0
. Thus
(r_{1}(a),-r_{2}(a))\in\im(r)\subset\overline{\mathrm{E}}
, and this element is primitive by the same argument that we used to prove (1).

To complete the proof of (5), as both
\Psi
and
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
have rank
2
, it suffices to find an element of
\Psi
that is not in
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
. Consider
(0,-r_{2}(a))\in\Psi
. Assume for a contradiction that some multiple of this is equal to
r(v)
, for
v\in\mathrm{E}
. By the formula above, this can only be true if
r_{2}(v)=kr_{2}(a)
, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
. But
r_{2}
is linear and injective by Lemma
2.19
, so we must have
v=ka
. It then follows from our computation above that
r(ka)=(kr_{1}(a),-kr_{2}(a))
; this is a contradiction.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
2.26
(2), we see that the left and right orthogonal complements of
\Psi
in
\mathrm{G}
agree. We denote this orthogonal complement by
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}
. The following lemma describes its properties.

Lemma 2.27
.

Equipping
\Psi_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}
with the bilinear form induced from
-\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

(
note the negative sign
)
, there is a natural isomorphism of lattices

\Psi_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}/\Psi\cong\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\oplus\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2}),

where
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
is equipped with its usual bilinear form
q(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Proof.

Recall that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
, the point-like element in
\mathrm{G}_{i}
is given by
r_{i}(a)
, so
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i}):=(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}/r_{i}(a)
.

We begin by showing that
(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
is a sublattice of
\mathrm{G}
in the obvious way. To do this we need to show that the bilinear forms induced on
(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
from those on
\mathrm{G}_{1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
, respectively from
\mathrm{G}
, agree.

Let
u,v\in(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
and write
u=(u_{1},u_{2})
,
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
. Then

\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}=\langle u_{1},v_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}+\langle u_{2},v_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}+\langle f_{1}(u_{1}),-f_{2}(v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}.

To show that this agrees with the bilinear form induced from those on
\mathrm{G}_{1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}
, we need to show that
\langle f_{1}(u_{1}),-f_{2}(v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
. As
u_{1}\in(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}
, we have
0=\langle u_{1},r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}=\langle f_{1}(u_{1}),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}
. By the definition of the bilinear form on
\mathrm{E}
, it follows that
f_{1}(u_{1})
must be an integer multiple of
a
. A similar argument shows that
f_{2}(v_{2})
must also be an integer multiple of
a
, so
\langle f_{1}(u_{1}),-f_{2}(v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=c\,\langle a,a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
, for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, as required.

Next we show that
\Psi_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}=(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
as subsets of
\mathrm{G}
. For any
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathrm{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle(v_{1},v_{2}),\,(r_{1}(a),0)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle v_{1},r_{1}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},

\displaystyle\langle(0,r_{2}(a)),\,(v_{1},v_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle r_{2}(a),v_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{2}},

so
v\in\Psi_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}
if and only if
v_{1}\in(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}
and
v_{2}\in(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
.

We thus have an equality of sublattices
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}=(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
of
\mathrm{G}
. We conclude by quotienting by
\Psi
and noting that the bilinear form
q(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
is induced by
-\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
2.26
, we also see that
(r_{1}(a),0)
and
(0,r_{2}(a))
both descend to the same primitive element
\mathbf{p}\in\mathrm{M}
.

Proposition 2.28
.

\mathrm{M}
is a surface-like pseudolattice with point-like element
\mathbf{p}
. The Néron-Severi group of
\mathrm{M}
, defined by
\NS(\mathrm{M}):=\mathbf{p}^{\perp}/\mathbf{p}
, fits into the following exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\zeta\longrightarrow\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\longrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})\longrightarrow 0,

where
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
\Psi
in
\mathrm{K}
and
\zeta
is a primitive generator of
\overline{\mathrm{E}}/r(a)
.

If
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
is equipped with the bilinear form induced by the natural inclusion

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\subset\Psi_{\mathrm{G}}^{\perp}/\Psi\cong\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\oplus\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2}),

\NS(\mathrm{M})
is equipped with its usual bilinear form
q(\cdot,\cdot)
, and
\zeta
is isotropic
(
\zeta.\zeta=0
)
, then the above sequence becomes an exact sequence of lattices.

Proof.

Note first that
\zeta
is well-defined as
r(a)
is primitive in
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
, so
\overline{\mathrm{E}}/r(a)
is a torsion-free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
1
.

The element
\mathbf{p}
is isotropic in
\mathrm{M}
, as the bilinear form on
\mathrm{M}
is induced from that on
\mathrm{K}
and
\Psi
is isotropic in
\mathrm{K}
. So, by
[
Kuz17
, Lemma 3.3(a)]
,
\mathrm{M}
is surface-like with point-like vector
\mathbf{p}
.

To prove that the given sequence is exact, we first observe that, by Lemma
2.26
, we have an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}r(a)\longrightarrow\Psi\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\mathbf{p}\longrightarrow 0,

and the restriction of the bilinear forms to the terms of this sequence are all identically zero. Moreover, as
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
is orthogonal to
\Psi
(by Lemma
2.23
), we have
\overline{\mathrm{E}}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}
, so we obtain a second exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\overline{\mathrm{E}}\longrightarrow\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{p}^{\perp}\longrightarrow 0,

and this is an exact sequence of lattices if we equip its terms with the induced bilinear forms from
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
,
\mathrm{G}
, and
\mathrm{M}
. Taking the quotient of the second sequence by the first, using the snake lemma, and flipping the signs on the bilinear forms gives the result.
∎

To conclude this section, the following lemma describes the image of
\mathbb{Z}\zeta
inside
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
.

Lemma 2.29
.

The inclusion
\mathbb{Z}\zeta\hookrightarrow\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
realises
\mathbb{Z}\zeta
as a totally degenerate rank
1
sublattice of
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\subset\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\oplus\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
. This sublattice contains the nonzero element
(-K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})
.

Proof.

The first statement follows immediately from Lemma
2.23
. For the second statement, note that
r(b)\in\overline{\mathrm{E}}\subset\Psi_{\mathrm{K}}^{\perp}
and that, by Equation (
1
), we have
r(b)=(r_{1}T_{f_{2}}(b),-r_{2}(b))
.
By Proposition
2.14
, we have
T_{f_{2}}(b)=-d_{2}a+b
, where
d_{2}:=q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
r(b)=(-d_{2}r_{1}(a)+r_{1}(b),-r_{2}(b))
,
which is congruent to
(r_{1}(b),-r_{2}(b))
modulo
\Psi
. The result follows by descending to the quotient and noting that, by Proposition
2.14
, we have
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=-[r_{i}(b)]
, where the square brackets
[-]
denote the class in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
.
∎

2.5.
Lattice polarisations

We define various notions of lattice polarisation on our Néron-Severi lattices.

Definition 2.30
.

(1)

A
lattice polarisation
on
\NS(\mathrm{M})
is defined to be a primitive sublattice
L\subset\NS(\mathrm{M})
.

(2)

A
lifted polarisation
on
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
is a primitive sublattice
\hat{L}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
such that
\zeta\in\hat{L}
.

(3)

Given a lifted polarisation
\hat{L}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
, the
intersection polarisation
on
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
is defined to be
L_{i}:=\hat{L}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. Note that
L_{i}
is primitive in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
.

Our first result shows that lattice polarisations and lifted polarisations are two sides of the same coin.

Lemma 2.31
.

If
L\subset\NS(\mathrm{M})
is a lattice polarisation, then the preimage of
L
under the map
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
is a lifted polarisation. Conversely, if
\hat{L}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
is a lifted polarisation, then the image of
\hat{L}
under the map
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
is a lattice polarisation.

Proof.

The first statement is obvious from the definitions. For the converse, we need to check that the image of
\hat{L}
is primitive in
\NS(\mathrm{M})
. Let
\varphi
denote the map
\varphi\colon\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
. Suppose that
v\in\NS(\mathrm{M})
is a primitive element such that
mv\in\varphi(\hat{L})
, for some integer
m>1
. As
\varphi
is surjective, we may find
w\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
such that
\varphi(w)=v
. Then
mw+n\zeta\in\hat{L}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, as
\varphi(mw)=mv\in\varphi(\hat{L})
and
\zeta
generates the kernel of
\varphi
. Now, as
\zeta\in\hat{L}
, it follows that
mw\in\hat{L}
. But
\hat{L}
is primitive, so we must also have
w\in\hat{L}
, and hence
\varphi(w)=v\in\varphi(\hat{L})
and
\varphi(\hat{L})
is primitive in
\NS(\mathrm{M})
.
∎

Next we look at the properties of the intersection polarisation.

Lemma 2.32
.

We have

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}.

In particular, if
\hat{L}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
is a lifted polarisation, then the corresponding intersection polarisation on
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
satisfies
{L}_{i}\subset K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}

Proof.

Begin by noting that, by Proposition
2.14
, we have
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=-[r_{i}(b)]
, where the square brackets
[-]
denote the class in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
.

Now, if
v\in\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})=(r_{i}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}/r_{i}(a)
is any class, let
\hat{v}\in(r_{i}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
denote a representative of
v
. From the proof of Lemma
2.27
we have
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}=(r_{1}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}\oplus(r_{2}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}
, so
\hat{v}\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}\subset\mathrm{G}
. Therefore, by definition of
f
, we have
f(\hat{v})=(-1)^{i-1}f_{i}(\hat{v})
. Thus, in particular, we find

q(v,K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}})=\langle\hat{v},r_{i}(b)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=\langle f_{i}(\hat{v}),b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=(-1)^{i-1}\langle f(\hat{v}),b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}.

Now suppose that
v\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. Then
\hat{v}\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}\subset\mathrm{K}
, so
q(v,K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}})=(-1)^{i-1}\langle f(\hat{v}),b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
,
as
\mathrm{K}=\ker(f)
. So
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})\subset K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
.

Conversely, suppose that
v\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
. Then

\displaystyle\langle f(\hat{v}),b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i-1}q(v,K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}})=0,

\displaystyle\langle f(\hat{v}),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i-1}\langle f_{i}(\hat{v}),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=(-1)^{i-1}\langle\hat{v},r_{i}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=0

as
\hat{v}\in(r_{i}(a))^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
. Therefore we must have
f(\hat{v})=0
, so
\hat{v}\in\mathrm{K}
. As we also have
\hat{v}\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}
, it follows that
\hat{v}\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}
, and so
v\in\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
. Thus
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
and the proof is complete.
∎

Unfortunately, in general the intersection polarisations
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
are not sufficient to determine the corresponding lattice/lifted polarisation. However, we can recover some information in many cases.

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
\varphi_{i}\colon K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
denote the morphism induced by the morphism
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
from Proposition
2.28
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, and let
\varphi:=\varphi_{1}\oplus\varphi_{2}\colon K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
. Then the
\varphi_{i}
are injective and moreover:

•

If
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
, then
\varphi
is injective. Its image is a finite index sublattice of
\NS(\mathrm{M})
.

•

If
\deg(\mathrm{G})=0
, then
\zeta\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
and
\ker(\varphi)=\mathbb{Z}\zeta
.

In particular,
\varphi_{i}
realises
L_{i}
as a sublattice of
L
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and if
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
, then
\varphi
realises
L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
as a sublattice of
L
.

Proof.

Injectivity of
\varphi_{i}
follows from the fact that the kernel
\mathbb{Z}\zeta
of the morphism
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
has trivial intersection with
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
for each
i
.

So suppose that
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
. Let
(v_{1},v_{2})
and
(w_{1},w_{2})
be any two distinct elements of
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
and assume for a contradiction that
\varphi(v_{1},v_{2})=\varphi(w_{1},w_{2})
. Then
(v_{1},v_{2})
and
(w_{1},w_{2})
differ by an integer multiple of
\zeta
so, by Lemma
2.29
, we have
(v_{1},v_{2})=(w_{1}-cK_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},w_{2}+cK_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})
,
for some nonzero
c\in\mathbb{Q}
. Now, as
v_{1},w_{1}\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}
, we have

0=q(v_{1},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=q(w_{1},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})-cq(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=-cq(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}).

But this contradicts the assumption that
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
. Thus
\varphi
must be injective. The fact that the image is a finite index sublattice then follows if we note that both
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
and
\NS(\mathrm{M})
have rank
18
.

If
\deg(\mathrm{G})=0
, then
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}})=0
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, so
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
. It follows from Lemma
2.29
that
\mathbb{Z}\zeta\subset K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
, which is the kernel of the morphism
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

Using the fact that
\NS(\mathrm{M})\cong H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
(Proposition
3.17
), which is unimodular, and the discriminant groups of
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
computed in Lemma
2.20
, one may show that the index of
\im(\varphi)
in
\NS(\mathrm{M})
is equal to
3
if
\deg(\mathrm{G})=9
, equal to
4
if
\deg(\mathrm{G})=8^{\prime}
, and equal to
\deg(\mathrm{G})
if
1\leq\deg(\mathrm{G})\leq 8
. If
\deg(\mathrm{G})=0
, then the quotient
\NS(M)/\im(\varphi)
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module of rank
1
.

Definition 2.35
.

The quotient group
Q(L):=L\,/\,\varphi(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})
is called the
coupling group
of
L
.

The coupling group measures how far the lattice polarisation
L\subset\NS(\mathrm{M})
is from being induced by the two intersection polarisations
L_{i}\subset\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. It consists of elements of
L
that “cross between” or “couple” the part of
L
contained in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})
and the part of
L
contained in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
. It fits into an exact sequence of abelian groups

(3)

L_{1}\oplus L_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longrightarrow}}L\longrightarrow Q(L)\longrightarrow 0.

Note that if
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
then
\varphi
is injective, so in this case sequence (
3
) is also exact on the left.

Our next result shows that the coupling group can be used to measure the failure of orthogonal complements to descend to the intersection polarisation.

Proposition 2.36
.

Assume that
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
. Then the coupling group
Q(L)
is torsion if and only if
(L_{i})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}}=\hat{L}^{\perp}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
for each
i=\{1,2\}
, where
(L_{i})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
L_{i}
taken in
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
.

Proof.

Note first that, as the class
\zeta
is totally isotropic, without ambiguity we can take
\hat{L}^{\perp}
to be either the orthogonal complement to
\hat{L}
in
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
or the lift of
L^{\perp}
under
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
, as these both give the same sublattice of
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
. Note also that
L_{i}\subset\hat{L}
, so
\hat{L}^{\perp}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})\subset(L_{i})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}}
.

Now assume that
Q(L)
is torsion. For simplicity of notation, in this part of the proof we will assume
i=1
; the proof for
i=2
is identical. Let
v\in(L_{1})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}}\subset\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})
. Then
\varphi(v,0)\in\NS(\mathrm{M})
. Let
w\in L
. Then as
Q(L)
is torsion, there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
w=k\varphi(w_{1},w_{2})
for some
w_{i}\in L_{i}
. Then
q(\varphi(v,0),w)=q_{1}(v,w_{1})+q_{2}(0,w_{2})=0
as
v\in L_{i}^{\perp}
; here
q
denotes the bilinear form on
\NS(\mathrm{M})
and
q_{i}
denotes the bilinear form on
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. So
\varphi(v,0)\subset L^{\perp}
and consequently
(v,0)\in\hat{L}^{\perp}
. This proves that
\hat{L}^{\perp}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})=(L_{1})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}}

To prove the converse, assume that
Q(L)
is not torsion. Then
\rank(L_{1})+\rank(L_{2})<\rank(L)
. Taking orthogonal complements, we find that

\rank\left((L_{1})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}}\right)+\rank\left((L_{2})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}}\right)>\rank\left(L^{\perp})\right.

But then we cannot have
(L_{i})^{\perp}_{K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}}=\hat{L}^{\perp}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
for both
i=\{1,2\}
.
∎

We conclude this section with a result about automorphisms of Néron-Severi lattices, which will be useful in Section
5
.

Proposition 2.37
.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, suppose that
\psi_{i}
is an isometry of
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
which fixes
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
. Then
\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}
induces an isometry
\psi
of
\NS(\mathrm{M})
which satisfies
\psi(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
(where we identify
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
with its image under the injective map
\varphi_{i}
from Lemma
2.33
).

Conversely, suppose that
\psi
is an isometry of
\NS(\mathrm{M})
satisfying
\psi(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp})=K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Then, after possibly multiplying
\psi
by
(-1)
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
there are isometries
\psi_{i}
of
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
which fix
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
and induce
\psi
.

Proof.

To prove the first part we use the matrix representation from the proof of Proposition
2.21
. For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, choose bases for
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
, let
Q_{i}
denote the matrix of the intersection form and let
K_{i}
denotes the column vector representing
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
with respect to this basis. Let
P_{i}
denote the matrix of
\psi_{i}
; note that
P_{i}^{T}Q_{i}P_{i}=Q_{i}
and
P_{i}K_{i}=P_{i}
by assumption. Let
P
denote the matrix

P=\begin{pmatrix}P_{1}&0\\
0&P_{2}\end{pmatrix}

of
\psi_{1}\oplus\psi_{2}
.

From the proof of Proposition
2.21
, the map
f
induces the map
F\colon\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\oplus\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})\to\mathrm{E}
given by
F(v_{1},v_{2})=(-K_{1}^{T}Q_{1}v_{1}+K_{2}^{T}Q_{2}v_{2})a
, where
a
is one of the standard basis elements
(a,b)
for
\mathrm{E}
. An easy matrix calculation then shows that
FP=F
. It follows that
F
induces an isometry on
\ker(F)=\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
. This isometry fixes
\zeta=c(-K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})
(for some
c\in\mathbb{Q}
), so further descends to an isometry of
\NS(\mathrm{M})
.

To prove the converse, assume first that
\deg(\mathrm{G})\neq 0
. Then
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}
and
K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
are non-degenerate by Lemma
2.20
. The map
\psi
induces isometries of the lattices
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
, which descend to automorphisms of the discriminant groups
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp})
that preserve the quadratic forms. These in turn induce an automorphism
\overline{\psi}
of
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp})\cong\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp})\oplus\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp})
.

Let
A:=\NS(\mathrm{M})\,/\,(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp})
. Then
A
is a subgroup of
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp})
which is isotropic with respect to the quadratic form, and the order of
A
is the index of
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
in
\NS(\mathrm{M})
. By
[
Nik80
, Proposition 1.4.2]
, the automorphism
\overline{\psi}
must preserve
A
.

Now, using the explicit descriptions of the discriminant groups from Lemma
2.20
, one may compute that the only automorphisms of
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp})
preserving
A
and the quadratic forms are
\pm\mathrm{Id}
. After multiplying
\psi
by
(-1)
if necessary, we may therefore assume that
\overline{\psi}=\mathrm{Id}
.

In particular,
\psi
acts trivially on
\disc(K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By
[
Dol12
, Proposition 8.2.5]
, it follows that
\psi
extends to isometries
\psi_{i}
of
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
which fix
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}
, as required.

Finally, assume that
\deg(\mathrm{G})=0
. Then
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\cap K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{Z}
is preserved by
\psi
. Let
\alpha\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\cap K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
be a primitive generator. After multiplying by
(-1)
if necessary, we may assume that
\psi(\alpha)=\alpha
. Consequently, we may assume that
\varphi_{1}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=\varphi_{2}(K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})\in K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}^{\perp}\cap K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}}^{\perp}
is fixed by
\psi
.

The class
\alpha
is totally isotropic in
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and, by Lemma
2.20
, we have a (non-unique) isometry
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{Z}\alpha\oplus E_{8}
. After fixing a choice of such an isometry, we may extend a standard basis for
\mathbb{Z}\alpha\oplus E_{8}
to a basis for
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})\cong\mathrm{I}_{1,9}\cong\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\oplus E_{8}
by adding a
(-1)
-class
\beta
, so that
\{\alpha,\beta\}
forms a standard basis for the
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}
factor.

To prove the result it suffices to show that any isometry
\psi
of
K_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}^{\perp}
with
\psi(\alpha)=\alpha
extends to an isometry
\psi_{i}
of
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. To do this it suffices to find
\psi_{i}(\beta)
. But it is a straightforward linear algebra computation to show that
\psi_{i}(\beta)
must be the unique
(-1)
-class which is orthogonal to
\psi_{i}(E_{8})
and has
q_{i}(\alpha,\psi_{i}(\beta))=1
(where
q_{i}
denotes the bilinear form on
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
).
∎

3.
Tyurin Degenerations of K3 Surfaces

Throughout this section we use the following notation. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
denote a semistable Type II degeneration of K3 surfaces over the open unit disc in
\mathbb{C}
; note in particular that as part of the Type II condition we assume that
K_{\mathcal{X}}\sim 0
. Let
X\subset\mathcal{X}
denote a general fibre and let
X_{p}\subset\mathcal{X}
denote the fibre over a point
p\in\Delta
. We assume further that
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a
Tyurin degeneration
, meaning that the central fibre
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
is a
stable K3 surface of Type II
in the sense of the following definition, originally due to Friedman. We let
i_{j}\colon C\to V_{j}
denote the inclusion.

Definition 3.1
.

[
Fri84
, Definition 3.1]
A
stable K3 surface of Type II
is a surface with normal crossings
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
consisting of two smooth rational surfaces
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
glued along a smooth anticanonical elliptic curve
C
, satisfying
N_{C/V_{1}}\otimes N_{C/V_{2}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that the normal bundle condition implies that if
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
is a stable K3 surface of Type II and
q
denotes the bilinear form on
\NS(V_{i})
, then
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})=-q(K_{V_{2}},K_{V_{2}})
. In line with our convention in Section
2.3
, we will henceforth assume that labels have been chosen so that
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})\geq 0
.

Remark 3.3
.

Tyurin degenerations are in some sense primitive in the class of Type II degenerations: given a Type II degeneration of polarised K3 surfaces, after twisting the polarisation and performing some birational modifications on the central fibre, one may obtain a Tyurin degeneration. We refer the interested reader to
[
Fri84
, Theorems 2.2 and 2.3]
for details.

3.1.
Hodge theory

We begin by studying the Hodge theory of such degenerations. The material in this section largely follows from work of Friedman
[
Fri84
]
and Scattone
[
Sca87
]
.

In the limiting mixed Hodge structure
H^{2}_{\lim}(X)
, the weight filtration is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
and is given by

0\subset I\subset I^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}),

where
I\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is a primitive rank two isotropic sublattice, the choice of which is unique up to isometries of
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
(see
[
Sca87
, Section 2.2]
).

Lemma 3.4
.

The quotient
I^{\perp}/I
is isomorphic to the lattice
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
, where
H
denotes the even unimodular lattice of signature
(1,1)
(the hyperbolic plane) and
E_{8}
denotes the usual negative definite root lattice.

Proof.

H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to the K3 lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K3}}:=H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. Note that each copy of
H
contains a primitive isotropic vector. As
I\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is unique up to isometry, without loss of generality we may take
I
to be generated by taking one primitive isotropic vector from each of the first two factors of
H
. It follows that the quotient
I^{\perp}/I
is isomorphic to
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
.
∎

With this notation, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5
.

The Clemens-Schmid exact sequence induces exact sequences over
\mathbb{Z}
as follows:

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow H^{0}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\longrightarrow 0,

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\xi\longrightarrow\xi^{\perp}\longrightarrow I^{\perp}/I\longrightarrow 0,

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\eta\longrightarrow H^{4}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{4}_{\lim}(X)\longrightarrow 0,

where

•

\xi\in H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})
is the class of
(C,-C)
,

•

\eta\in H^{4}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{4}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})
is the class of
(p,-p)
, for
p
a point.

Proof.

The difficult part of this proposition, namely the second sequence, follows immediately from a result of Friedman
[
Fri84
, Lemma 3.5]
. We follow Friedman’s approach to prove exactness of the remaining two sequences.

Exactness of these sequences follows from the fact that the Clemens-Schmid sequence is obtained by splicing together the Wang sequence

\ldots\to H^{i-1}(X;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{i}(X;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{i}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to\cdots

and the exact sequence of a pair

\ldots\to H^{i}_{V_{0}}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{i}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{*};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{i+1}_{V_{0}}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{Z})\to\cdots,

where
\mathcal{X}^{*}=\mathcal{X}\setminus X_{0}
. When
i=0
or
i=4
we have
H^{i-1}(X,\mathbb{Z})=0
and
N=0
, so the Wang sequence gives an isomorphism

H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{*},\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{i}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Furthermore, we have
H^{i}_{X_{0}}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i=0,1,5
, which completes the proof of the first sequence. To conclude the proof for the third sequence, note that the image of the map
H^{4}_{X_{0}}(\mathcal{X};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{4}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the class
\eta
.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that, by
[
Fri84
, Lemma 3.5]
, the second sequence here arises from the second weight graded piece of the Clemens-Schmid sequence

\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{X_{0}}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})\longrightarrow\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}(X)\longrightarrow 0,

where the class
\xi
generates the image of the first map and
\xi^{\perp}=\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})
.

Using the viewpoint of perverse and weight filtrations
P_{\bullet}
and
W_{\bullet}
described in
[
DT21
, Sections 4.1 and 5.2.1]
, the classes
\xi
and
\eta
are generators of the graded pieces
\Gr_{2}^{W}\Gr_{2}^{P}H^{2}(X_{0})
and
\Gr_{4}^{W}\Gr_{4}^{P}H^{4}(X_{0})
, respectively.

We may take the direct sum of the exact sequences from Proposition
3.5
to obtain an exact sequence over
\mathbb{Z}

(4)

0\to\mathbb{Z}\xi\oplus\mathbb{Z}\eta\to H^{0}(X_{0})\oplus\xi^{\perp}\oplus H^{4}(X_{0})\to H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)\to 0.

3.2.
Clemens-Schmid and pseudolattices over the rational numbers

Our next aim is to show how the results of Section
3.1
are naturally recovered from the abstract considerations in Section
2.3
, for quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms
f_{i}\colon\mathrm{G}_{i}\to\mathrm{E}
arising from the anticanonical pairs
(V_{i},C)
making up the central fibre. In this subsection we begin by working over the rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
; in the subsequent subsections we will discuss to what extent this continues to work over
\mathbb{Z}
.

For any surface
V
, it is well-known that the Chern character map

\mathrm{ch}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\longrightarrow H^{\mathrm{even}}(V;\mathbb{Q}):=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{2}H^{2i}(V;\mathbb{Q})

is a ring homomorphism. Moreover, it follows easily from
[
Kuz17
, Example 3.5]
that this map is injective and its
\mathbb{Q}
-linear extension induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{ch_{\mathbb{Q}}}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\otimes\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(V;\mathbb{Q})\oplus(\NS(V)\otimes\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{4}(V;\mathbb{Q}).

The Chern character maps the class of
\mathcal{O}_{V}
to
(1,0,0)
, the class of the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{p}
of a point to
(0,0,1)
, and the class of
\mathcal{O}_{V}(D)
, for any divisor
D
on
V
, to
(1,D,\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V}(D))+\frac{1}{2}q(K_{V},D)-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V}))
, where
q(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the usual bilinear form on
H^{2}(V;\mathbb{Q})
induced by the cup product; it follows from
[
Kuz17
, Example 3.5]
that these classes span the image of
\mathrm{ch}
.

Remark 3.7
.

Note that the Chern character map is
not
the same as the isomorphism
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus\NS(V)\oplus\mathbb{Z}
from
[
Kuz17
, Example 3.5]
that we used in the proof of Proposition
2.21

Using this, it is easy to show that the Euler form on
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))
is induced by the bilinear form on
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V;\mathbb{Q})
with matrix

(5)

\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}K^{T}Q$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}QK$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-Q$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]$}},

where
Q
denotes the matrix of the bilinear form
q
with respect to an appropriate choice of basis for
H^{2}(V;\mathbb{Q})
and
K
denotes the column vector representing the canonical divisor
K_{V}
in this basis.

Suppose that
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
is a stable K3 surface of Type II. Each of the surfaces
V_{j}
is a quasi del Pezzo surface. So, by Example
2.16
, the derived pull-back map
i_{j}^{*}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))\to\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism of pseudolattices, with right adjoint given by derived push-forward
i_{j*}
. The class of the structure sheaf of a point defines a point-like element in
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))
and with respect to this point-like element we have
\NS(\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j})))\cong\NS(V_{j})\cong H^{2}(V_{j};\mathbb{Z})
. This isomorphism identifies the canonical classes, so by Remark
3.2
we have
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})=-q(K_{V_{2}},K_{V_{2}})
. It follows that we are in the setting of Section
2.3
, with
\mathrm{G}_{j}=\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j})))
and
f_{j}=i_{j}^{*}
.

Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
denote the spherical homomorphism obtained by gluing the pseudolattices
\mathrm{G}_{1}=\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{1}))
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}=\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{2}))
along
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))\cong\mathrm{E}
via the homomorphisms
i_{1}^{*}
and
(-i_{2}^{*})
, as in Section
2.3
, and let
r
be its right adjoint. The results of Section
2.3
give rise to lattices
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
,
\mathrm{K}
, and
\mathrm{M}
, related by the short exact sequence (
2
).

Lemma 3.8
.

The Chern character map induces a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules

\kappa\colon\mathrm{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{1};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{2};\mathbb{Q}),

where
\mathrm{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathrm{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. The restriction of
\kappa
to
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathrm{K}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
induces an isomorphism

\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}(X_{0};\mathbb{Q})\oplus\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{4}(X_{0};\mathbb{Q}),

where
W_{\bullet}
is the weight filtration arising from Deligne’s mixed Hodge structure on a normal crossing variety.

Proof.

As
H^{2}(V_{j};\mathbb{Z})\cong\NS(V_{j})
, the Chern character induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{j};\mathbb{Q})
. Under this identification, the bilinear form
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))}
is given by Equation (
5
) and the maps
i_{j}^{*}
and
i_{j*}
are given by

\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{p}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-K_{j}^{T}Q_{j}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{C}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]

and

\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{p}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{C}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-K_{j}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}q(K_{V_{j}},K_{V_{j}})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]

respectively, where
Q_{j}
denotes the matrix of the usual bilinear form on
H^{2}(V_{j};\mathbb{Q})
with respect to an appropriate choice of basis, and
K_{j}
denotes the column vector representing the canonical divisor
K_{V_{j}}
in this basis.

We may thus identify
\mathrm{G}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
with
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{1};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{2};\mathbb{Q})
as
\mathbb{Q}
-modules, proving the first statement. The bilinear form on
\mathrm{G}
induces a bilinear form on
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{1};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{2};\mathbb{Q})
with matrix

\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\

5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}Q_{1}K_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-Q_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-Q_{1}K_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
