5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}K_{2}^{T}Q_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}Q_{2}K_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-Q_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]$}}.

Now recall that
f(u_{1},u_{2})=i_{1}^{*}(u_{1})-i_{2}^{*}(u_{2})
for
u_{i}\in\mathrm{G}_{i}
and, by the proof of
[
HT20
, Proposition 2.23]
,
r(v)=(i_{1*}(v)-i_{1*}i_{2}^{*}i_{2*}(v),-i_{2*}(v))
for
v\in\mathrm{E}
. The maps
f
and
r
from Section
2.3
are therefore given respectively by

\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{p}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-K_{1}^{T}Q_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle K_{2}^{T}Q_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{C}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]

and

\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{p}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle{[\mathcal{O}_{C}]}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-K_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{1})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{2}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle K_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}(V_{2})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\frac{1}{2}q(K_{V_{2}},K_{V_{2}})$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]$}}.

If we write a general vector in
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{j};\mathbb{Q})
as
(r_{j},D_{j},s_{j})
, it follows that the kernel
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
f
is identified with the subspace of
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{1};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{2};\mathbb{Q})
defined by

\{(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})\mid r_{1}=r_{2}\text{ and }q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})\}.

Now, Deligne’s mixed Hodge structure on a normal crossing variety gives that

\displaystyle H^{0}(X_{0})

\displaystyle\cong\{(r_{1},r_{2})\in H^{0}(V_{1})\oplus H^{0}(V_{2})\mid r_{1}=r_{2}\},

\displaystyle\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})

\displaystyle\cong\{(D_{1},D_{2})\in H^{2}(V_{1})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2})\mid q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})\},

\displaystyle H^{4}(X_{0})

\displaystyle\cong H^{4}(V_{1})\oplus H^{4}(V_{2}),

so we see that
H^{0}(X_{0})\oplus\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})\oplus H^{4}(X_{0})\cong\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, as required. ∎

Notation 3.9
.

In light of the isomorphisms from this lemma, we will write elements of
\mathrm{G}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as
6
-tuples

(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2}),

where
r_{i}\in H^{0}(V_{i};\mathbb{Q})
,
D_{i}\in H^{2}(V_{i},\mathbb{Q})
, and
s_{i}\in H^{4}(V_{i};\mathbb{Q})
. By the proof of this lemma, we can express elements of the submodule
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as
6
-tuples as above satisfying the two conditions
r_{1}=r_{2}
and
q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})
.

Now suppose that
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
occurs as the central fibre of a Tyurin degeneration
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
. Then the following result gives an interpretation of the lattice
\mathrm{M}
.

Lemma 3.10
.

There is a natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules

\mu\colon\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}(X)\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X),

where
\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\mathrm{M}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
W_{\bullet}
is the weight filtration arising from the limiting mixed Hodge structure. Moreover, under the isomorphisms
\kappa
,
\mu
the map
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
from the short exact sequence (
2
) agrees with the one induced by the Clemens-Schmid exact sequence (
4
).

Proof.

By Proposition
3.5
and Remark
3.6
, the Clemens-Schmid exact sequence gives rise to an isomorphism
\varphi_{0}\colon H^{0}(X_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}H^{0}_{\lim}(X)
, along with surjective maps
\varphi_{2}\colon\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})\to\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}(X)
and
\varphi_{4}\colon H^{4}(X_{0})\to H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
. Composing
\varphi:=\varphi_{0}\oplus\varphi_{2}\oplus\varphi_{4}
with the isomorphism
\kappa
from Lemma
3.8
, we thus obtain a surjective map of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules

\varphi\circ\kappa\colon\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}(X)\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X).

To prove the proposition, it suffices to show that the kernel of
\varphi\circ\kappa
is isomorphic to
\overline{\mathrm{E}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}=\im(r)\otimes\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

From the proof of Lemma
3.8
, we see that
\overline{\mathrm{E}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\subset\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is generated by the columns of the matrix of the map
r
. Writing a general element of
\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}
as
(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})
, and recalling that
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})=-q(K_{V_{2}},K_{V_{2}})
, we may simplify this set of generators to
\{(0,0,1,0,0,-1),(0,-K_{V_{1}},0,0,K_{V_{2}},0)\}
.

By Proposition
3.5
, we see that
\varphi_{0}
is an isomorphism and the kernels of
\varphi_{2}
and
\varphi_{4}
are generated by the classes
\xi=(-K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{2}})\in\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}(X_{0})
and
\eta=(1,-1)\in H^{4}(X_{0})
respectively. It thus follows immediately that
\ker(\varphi)=\overline{\mathrm{E}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
; this completes the proof.∎

Putting everything in this subsection together, we have the following result, which gives a relationship between the sequences (
2
) and (
4
) over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be a Tyurin degeneration with central fibre
X_{0}
a stable K3 surface of Type II. Then there is a commutative diagram of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrm{E}}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}\xi\oplus\mathbb{Q}\eta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(X_{0})\oplus\xi^{\perp}\oplus H^{4}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

where the vertical arrows are isomorphisms.

3.3.
Integrality

Next we investigate to what extent Proposition
3.11
holds over the integers. We note first that the map
\kappa
is not integral: indeed, let
D_{1}\in\NS(V_{1})\cong H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})
and
D_{2}\in\NS(V_{2})\cong H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})
. Then
(\mathcal{O}_{V_{1}}(D_{1}),\mathcal{O}_{V_{2}}(D_{2}))
is an integral element of
\mathrm{G}
. Applying the map
\kappa
gives

\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{V_{1}}(D_{1}),\mathcal{O}_{V_{2}}(D_{2}))=(1,D_{1},s_{1},1,D_{2},s_{2})

with

\displaystyle s_{1}

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V_{1}}(D_{1}))+\tfrac{1}{2}q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V_{1}}),

\displaystyle s_{2}

\displaystyle=\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V_{2}}(D_{2}))+\tfrac{1}{2}q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})-\chi(\mathcal{O}_{V_{2}}).

From these expressions we see that
\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{V_{1}}(D_{1}),\mathcal{O}_{V_{2}}(D_{2}))
is not integral unless
q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})
and
q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})
are even. The following lemma shows that this is, essentially, the only way in which integrality can fail.

Lemma 3.12
.

The map
\kappa
induces an isomorphism between
\mathrm{G}
and the subset of
H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{1};\mathbb{Q})\oplus H^{\mathrm{even}}(V_{2};\mathbb{Q})
defined by those
6
-tuples
(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})
satisfying

•

r_{i}\in H^{0}(V_{i};\mathbb{Z})
for each
i=1,2
;

•

D_{i}\in H^{2}(V_{i};\mathbb{Z})
for each
i=1,2
;

•

2s_{i}\in H^{4}(V_{i};\mathbb{Z})
for each
i=1,2
, and
s_{i}\in H^{4}(V_{i};\mathbb{Z})
if and only if
q(K_{V_{i}},D_{i})
is even.

This isomorphism takes
\mathrm{K}
to the set of
6
-tuples as above satisfying
r_{1}=r_{2}
and
q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})
.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of the explicit description of the Chern character map given at the start of Section
3.2
.
∎

Whilst the isomorphism
\kappa
is not integral, somewhat surprisingly we find that the isomorphism
\mu
is.

Lemma 3.13
.

The map
\mu\colon\mathrm{M}\to H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
is an isomorphism over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We first note that the top and bottom rows of the commutative diagram in the statement of Proposition
3.11
are defined over
\mathbb{Z}
, by the results of Sections
2.3
and
3.1
. By commutativity of this diagram, it suffices to show that the composition
\varphi\circ\kappa\colon\mathrm{K}\to H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
is well-defined and surjective over
\mathbb{Z}
.

The map
\varphi
is given explicitly by

\varphi(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})=(r_{1},D_{1}+D_{2},s_{1}+s_{2})\in H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X).

Combining this with the description of the image of
\kappa
from Lemma
3.12
, we find that the image of
\varphi\circ\kappa
is integral in
H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
, so
\varphi\circ\kappa
is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
.

It remains to show that
\varphi\circ\kappa
is surjective. Indeed, as the map
\varphi
is surjective over
\mathbb{Z}
, for any
x\in H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
there exists

y=(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})\in H^{0}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})\oplus\xi^{\perp}\oplus H^{4}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})

with
\varphi(y)=x
. To complete the proof, note that if
y\notin\kappa(\mathrm{K})
, we may use the description
\kappa(\mathrm{K})
from Lemma
3.12
and the fact that

\varphi(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2})=\varphi(r_{1},D_{1},s_{1}+\tfrac{1}{2},r_{2},D_{2},s_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}),

to obtain an element of
\kappa(\mathrm{K})
mapping to
x
.
∎

As noted in Section
2.3
, the bilinear form on
\mathrm{K}
descends under the map
\mathrm{K}\to\mathrm{M}
to give a bilinear form on
\mathrm{M}
. However,
H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
also comes equipped with a bilinear form, induced by the bilinear form on
H^{\mathrm{even}}_{\lim}(X)\cong H^{\mathrm{even}}(X;\mathbb{Z})
defined by Equation (
5
) (which is integral as
K_{X}=0
). The following result shows that these bilinear forms agree.

Lemma 3.14
.

Equipping
\mathrm{M}
and
H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
with bilinear forms as above, the map
\mu
is an isomorphism of lattices.

Proof.

Using Equation (
5
) and the explicit computations in the proofs of Lemmas
3.8
and
3.10
, one may show that both bilinear forms are given explicitly by the matrix

\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{0}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle H^{4}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{0}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 2$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-Q$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle H^{4}_{\lim}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]

where
Q
denotes the matrix induced by the cup-product form on
\Gr_{2}^{W}H^{2}_{\lim}(X)
with respect to an appropriate basis. ∎

Putting this lemma together with the exact sequence (
2
), we thus obtain the following result.

Proposition 3.15
.

In the context of a Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, the exact sequence (
2
) can be realised as

0\longrightarrow\overline{\mathrm{E}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{K}\longrightarrow H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)\longrightarrow 0,

where

•

\overline{\mathrm{E}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the zero form,

•

\mathrm{K}
is the kernel of the spherical homomorphism
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
obtained by gluing
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{1}))
and
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{2}))
along
\mathrm{E}\cong\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
, as in Section
2.3
, and

•

the bilinear form on
H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
is induced by the bilinear form on
H^{\mathrm{even}}_{\lim}(X)\cong H^{\mathrm{even}}(X;\mathbb{Z})
defined by Equation (
5
).

3.4.
Point-like vectors and the Néron-Severi lattice

Next we ask how the ideas of Section
2.4
arise in this setting.

In this setting, by
[
HT20
, Example 3.10]
,
\Psi
is the sublattice of
\mathrm{K}
generated by the classes of
(r_{1}(a),0)=([\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}],0)
and
(0,r_{2}(a))=(0,[\mathcal{O}_{p_{2}}])
, for
p_{i}
a point on
X_{i}
. Using Notation
3.9
,
\Psi
is generated by
(0,0,1,0,0,0)
and
(0,0,0,0,0,1)
. By Lemma
2.27
, the lattice isomorphisms
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})\cong\NS(V_{i})\cong H^{2}(V_{i};\mathbb{Z})
induce an isomorphism of lattices

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}/\Psi\cong H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z}).

Using the description of the bilinear form on
\mathrm{G}
in the proof of Lemma
3.8
, we see that this induces an isomorphism of lattices

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cong\{(D_{1},D_{2})\in H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})\mid q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})\}=\xi^{\perp},

where
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}/\Psi
and
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
are equipped with the bilinear forms from Lemma
2.27
and Proposition
2.28
.

Remark 3.16
.

Note that these isomorphisms are
not
the ones induced by the map
\kappa
coming from the Chern character (see Lemma
3.8
). Indeed, by Lemma
3.12
, the image of
\kappa
may even contain some non-integral classes. Instead, they may be thought of as a composition of
\kappa
with a projection to the subspace
H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})
.

The map to
\mathrm{M}\cong H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
takes the generators
(0,0,1,0,0,0)
and
(0,0,0,0,0,1)
of
\Psi
to a generator
\mathbf{p}\in H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
. By the description of the bilinear form on
\mathrm{M}
from Lemma
3.14
, we see that the natural isomorphism
\mu\colon\mathrm{M}\to H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
induces an isomorphism
\NS(\mathrm{M})=\mathbf{p}^{\perp}/\mathbf{p}\cong I^{\perp}/I
.

Finally, by Lemma
2.29
, we see that the kernel of the map
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
is generated by
\zeta=(-K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{2}})=(C,-C)=\xi
. Putting things together, we thus obtain the following result.

Proposition 3.17
.

If
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
is a stable K3 surface of type II, then the lattice isomorphisms
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})\cong\NS(V_{i})
induce an isomorphism of lattices

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{G}}/\Psi\cong H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z}).

If
X_{0}
is the central fibre of a Tyurin degeneration
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
, then the isomorphism above induces an isomorphism between the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\zeta\longrightarrow\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\longrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})\longrightarrow 0

from Proposition
2.28
and the the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\xi\longrightarrow\xi^{\perp}\longrightarrow I^{\perp}/I\longrightarrow 0

from Proposition
3.5
. From Lemma
3.4
, it follows that
\NS(\mathrm{M})\cong H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
.

3.5.
Lattice polarisations

We now consider how the constructions we have discussed so far work in the presence of a lattice polarisation. We will make use of the following standard definition.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
L
be a non-degenerate lattice. A class
\delta\in L
with
\langle\delta,\delta\rangle_{L}=-2
is called a
root
. A subset
\Delta(L)^{+}
of
positive roots
is a set of roots with the following properties:

•

for every root
\delta\in L
, we either have
\delta\in\Delta(L)^{+}
or
-\delta\in\Delta(L)^{+}
, but not both;

•

if
\delta
is a root that can be written as a non-negative integer combination of classes from
\Delta(L)^{+}
, then
\delta\in\Delta(L)^{+}
.

Now suppose that
L
is a non-degenerate primitive sublattice of the K3 lattice
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}\cong H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
of signature
(1,\rank(L)-1)
. We next define the notion of a lattice polarisation on a K3 surface, following Alexeev’s and Engel’s
[
AE23
]
modification of Dolgachev’s
[
Dol96
]
original definition.

Begin by fixing a vector
h\in L\otimes\mathbb{R}
, such that
\langle h,h\rangle_{L}>0
and
h\notin L^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{R}
for any proper sublattice
L^{\prime}\subsetneq L
with
\rank(L^{\prime})<\rank(L)
; Alexeev and Engel call such vectors
very irrational
.

Definition 3.19
.

[
AE23
, Definition 2.6]
An
L
-quasipolarised K3 surface
is a K3 surface
X
along with a primitive lattice embedding
j\colon L\hookrightarrow\NS(X)
for which
j(h)
is nef and big.

Let
\Delta(L)^{+}
denote the set of classes

\Delta(L)^{+}:=\{\delta\in L\mid\langle\delta,\delta\rangle_{L}=-2,\ \langle\delta,h\rangle_{L}>0\}.

The fact that
h
is very irrational implies that
\Delta(L)^{+}
is a set of positive roots for
L
. Moreover, it follows from
[
Dol96
, Remark 1.1]
that if
X
is an
L
-quasipolarized K3 surface, then
j(\Delta(L)^{+})\subset\NS(X)
is precisely the set of classes of effective
(-2)
-curves contained in
j(L)
.

The following definition is based on
[
DHNT15
, Definition 2.1]
, appropriately modified for compatibility with Definition
3.19
. Compare also Alexeev’s and Engel’s definition of a
nef model

[
AE23
, Definition 3.10]
in the presence of a lattice polarisation.

Definition 3.20
.

A
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces
is a Tyurin degeneration
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
such that

(1)

(monodromy invariance) over the punctured disc
\Delta^{*}
there is a trivial local subsystem
\mathcal{L}
of
R^{2}\pi_{*}\mathbb{Z}
with fibre
L
, so that for each
p\in\Delta^{*}
the fibre
\mathcal{L}_{p}\subset H^{2}(X_{p};\mathbb{Z})
defines an
L
-quasipolarisation of
X_{p}
,

(2)

(positivity) there exists an effective, flat, and nef Cartier divisor
A
on
\mathcal{X}
such that the restriction
A_{p}
of
A
to any fibre
X_{p}
over
p\in\Delta^{*}
is nef and big.

Remark 3.21
.

We note that, given a Tyurin degeneration
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
along with a Cartier divisor
A
on
\mathcal{X}
such that the restriction
A_{p}
of
A
to any fibre
X_{p}
over
p\in\Delta^{*}
is nef and big, by
[
SB83
, Theorem 1]
one may twist
A
by a Cartier divisor
Z
supported on components of
X_{0}
and perform a series of flops to arrange that
A
is effective, flat, and nef on
\mathcal{X}
.

The following lemma relates Definition
3.20
to the description in terms of pseudolattices.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces and let
0\subset I\subset I^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})

denote the associated limiting mixed Hodge structure. Then
L
is orthogonal to
I
and there is an embedding
L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}/I
.

Proof.

From the definition of the limiting mixed Hodge structure, we have
I^{\perp}=\ker(N)\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, where
N
is the logarithm of the monodromy. As
L\subset H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is primitive and monodromy invariant, we have that
L
is a primitive sublattice of
\ker(N)=I^{\perp}
; in particular we see that
L
is orthogonal to
I
. Moreover,
I\subset I^{\perp}
is a totally degenerate sublattice and
L
is nondegenerate, so
L\cap I=0
. It follows that
L
embeds into
I^{\perp}/I
.
∎

Definition 3.23
.

A Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces is called
primitively
L
-polarised
if it is
L
-polarised and the embedding
L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}/I
from Lemma
3.22
is primitive.

Remark 3.24
.

It is not the case that all
L
-polarised Tyurin degenerations of K3 surfaces are primitively
L
-polarised. Indeed, from Shimada’s list of elliptically fibred K3 surfaces
[
Shi00
]
there exists an elliptically fibred K3 surface
X
with Néron-Severi lattice
H\oplus D_{16}
which embeds primitively into
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
(see
[
Shi05
]
for the full list, where this case is number 1903 with trivial Mordell-Weil group). However,
H\oplus D_{16}
does not embed primitively into
I^{\perp}/I\cong H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
; instead it is a sublattice of index
2
. Some conditions that are sufficient to ensure primitivity are explored in Section
3.6
.

Following the discussion of the previous section, composing the embedding
L\hookrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})
from Lemma
3.22
with the isomorphism
\NS(\mathrm{M})\cong I^{\perp}/I
gives an embedding
L\hookrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})
. If the Tyurin degeneration is primitively
L
-polarised, then we obtain a lattice polarisation on
\NS(\mathrm{M})
in the sense of Definition
2.30
(1).

We next define the notion of a lattice polarisation on a stable K3 surface of type II
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
; this definition generalises the notion of polarisation by a divisor from
[
Fri84
, Definition 3.11]
. Recall from Section
3.1
that we have an isomorphism

\Gr^{W}_{2}H^{2}(X_{0})\cong\xi^{\perp}=\{(D_{1},D_{2})\in H^{2}(V_{1})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2})\mid q(K_{V_{1}},D_{1})=q(K_{V_{2}},D_{2})\},

where
\xi
denotes the isotropic class
(-K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{2}})=(C,-C)
.

Definition 3.25
.

An
\hat{L}
-polarisation
on a stable K3 surface of Type II is a primitive sublattice
\hat{L}\subset\xi^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(V_{1};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(V_{2};\mathbb{Z})
satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\xi\in\hat{L}
;

(2)

\hat{L}
contains the class of a nef Cartier divisor
A
on
X_{0}
with strictly positive self-intersection;

(3)

For every
(-2)
-class
\delta\in\hat{L}
, either
\delta
or
-\delta
is congruent modulo
\xi
to the class of an effective Cartier divisor of self-intersection
(-2)
.

Following the discussion of the previous section, an
\hat{L}
-polarisation on a stable K3 surface of Type II defines a primitive embedding
\hat{L}\hookrightarrow\xi^{\perp}\cong\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
and the condition that
\xi\in\hat{L}
corresponds precisely to the condition that the image of this embedding contains the class
\zeta
. We therefore obtain a lifted polarisation on
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
in the sense of Definition
2.30
(2).

We can relate this to lattice polarised Tyurin degenerations as follows. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be a primitively
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces. Then
L
is a primitive sublattice of
I^{\perp}/I\cong\NS(\mathrm{M})
. Let
\hat{L}
denote the preimage of
L
under the map from
\xi^{\perp}\cong\Psi_{\mathrm{K}}^{\perp}/\Psi
. Then
\hat{L}
is the lifted polarisation determined by
L
, in the sense of Lemma
2.31
, and we have the following result.

Theorem 3.26
.

With notation as above, if
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a primitively
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, then the central fibre
X_{0}
of
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is an
\hat{L}
-polarised stable K3 surface of Type II.

Proof.

The discussion above shows that there is a primitive embedding of
\hat{L}
into
\xi^{\perp}
. The image of this embedding contains
\xi
, as
\xi
generates the kernel of the map
\xi^{\perp}\to I^{\perp}/I
. Denote this map by
\varphi
.

Let
A
denote the Cartier divisor from Definition
3.20
(2). Then the restriction of
A
to the central fibre
X_{0}
is a nef Cartier divisor with positive self-intersection that lies in
\hat{L}
.

Finally, Definition
3.20
(1) implies that effective
(-2)
-curves in
\Delta(L)^{+}\subset L
are monodromy invariant, so we may take flat limits to obtain an embedding of
\Delta(L)^{+}
into
\hat{L}\subset\xi^{\perp}
which commutes with
\varphi
. The image of this embedding consists of classes of effective Cartier divisors of self-intersection
(-2)
in
\hat{L}
. If
\delta
is any
(-2)
-class in
\hat{L}
, then either
\varphi(\delta)
or
-\varphi(\delta)
lies in
\Delta(L)^{+}\subset L
(as
\Delta(L)^{+}
is a system of positive roots in
L
) so, by the argument above, either
\delta
or
-\delta
is congruent modulo
\xi
to the class of an effective Cartier divisor of self-intersection
(-2)
.
∎

We conclude this section by showing that the definitions of lattice polarisation above are compatible with the definition of a lattice polarisation on a weak del Pezzo surface from
[
DT18
]
.

Definition 3.27
.

[
DT18
, Definition 4.4]
Let
V
be a weak del Pezzo surface and let
C\subset V
be a smooth anticanonical divisor. Let
N
be a negative definite lattice. An
N
-polarisation
of
(V,C)
is a primitive embedding
j\colon N\hookrightarrow\NS(V)
such that

•

j(v).C=0
for all
v\in N
, and

•

there exists a set of positive roots
\Delta(N)^{+}\subset N
such that
j(\Delta(N)^{+})
is contained in the effective cone in
\NS(V)
.

The following result shows that this is compatible with our definitions.

Theorem 3.28
.

Let
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
be an
\hat{L}
-polarised stable K3 surface of Type II and suppose that
V_{1}
is weak del Pezzo. Then the intersection polarisation
L_{1}:=\hat{L}\cap\NS(V_{1})
defines an
L_{1}
-polarisation of
(V_{1},C)
, in the sense of Definition
3.27
.

Proof.

The condition that
j(v).C=0
for all
v\in L_{1}
follows from Lemma
2.32
, and negative definiteness follows from the fact that
C^{\perp}=K_{V_{i}}^{\perp}\subset\NS(V_{1})
is negative definite for any weak del Pezzo
V_{1}
. To show the second condition from Definition
3.27
, it suffices to show that if
\delta\in L_{1}
is a class of self-intersection
(-2)
, then either
\delta
or
(-\delta)
is effective.

So let
\delta\in L_{1}
be a class of self-intersection
(-2)
. Write
\delta=(v,0)\in\NS(V_{1})\oplus\NS(V_{2})
. After possibly exchanging
\delta
for
(-\delta)
, by Definition
3.25
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\delta-k\xi=(v-kC,kC)
is effective. As
C
is an effective curve in
\NS(V_{2})
, this can only occur if
k\geq 0
. But then
v=(v-kC)+kC\in\NS(V_{1})
is a sum of effective classes, so is effective.
∎

Remark 3.29
.

The condition that
V_{1}
is weak del Pezzo holds if and only if
\deg(\mathrm{G})=q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})>0
. As our labelling convention ensures that
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})\geq 0
and we have
q(K_{V_{1}},K_{V_{1}})=-q(K_{V_{2}},K_{V_{2}})
by Remark
3.2
,
V_{2}
can never be weak del Pezzo, as the canonical divisor on any weak del Pezzo surface always has positive self-intersection.

Putting the results of this section together we obtain the following. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be a primitively
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces. Then
L
is a primitive sublattice of
I^{\perp}/I\cong\NS(\mathrm{M})
. If
\hat{L}\subset\xi^{\perp}\cong\Psi_{\mathrm{K}}^{\perp}/\Psi
is the lifted polarisation induced by
L
, in the sense of Definition
2.30
(2), then by Theorem
3.26
the central fibre
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
of
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is an
\hat{L}
-polarised stable K3 surface of Type II. Finally, assuming that
V_{1}
is weak del Pezzo, if
L_{1}\subset\NS(V_{1})\cong\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})
is the intersection polarisation, in the sense of Definition
2.30
(3), then by Theorem
3.28
we obtain an
L_{1}
-polarisation of
(V_{1},C)
, in the sense of
[
DT18
, Definition 4.4]
.

3.6.
Primitivity

In this section we explore sufficient conditions under which an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces is primitively
L
-polarised. As in the previous section, we let
L
be a non-degenerate primitive sublattice of the K3 lattice
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}\cong H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
of signature
(1,\rank({L})-1)
. Denote the bilinear form on
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}
and the orthogonal complement of
L
inside
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
by
L^{\perp}
.

The following definition is due to Dolgachev
[
Dol96
]
.

Definition 3.30
.

Let
L
be a nondegenerate lattice and
e\in L
be a primitive isotropic element. Define
\divop(e)
to be the positive generator of the image of the linear map
\langle e,-\rangle_{L}\colon L\to\mathbb{Z}
. Then
e
is called
m
-admissible in
L
if
\divop(e)=m
and there exists an isotropic class
g\in L
with
\divop(g)=m
and
\langle e,g\rangle_{L}=m
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces. By Lemma
3.22
we have that
I
is a primitive sublattice of
L^{\perp}
. This allows us to state the following definition.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
L
be a nondegenerate lattice and let
I\subset L
be a primitive rank two isotropic sublattice.
I
is called
doubly admissible in
L
if there exist primitive generators
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
for
I
and isotropic classes
g_{1},g_{2}\in L
such that
\langle e_{1},g_{1}\rangle_{L}=\langle e_{2},g_{2}\rangle_{L}=1
and
\langle e_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{L}=\langle e_{2},g_{1}\rangle_{L}=0
.

An
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces is called
doubly admissible
if
I
is doubly admissible in
L^{\perp}
.

The following lemma gives some equivalent conditions for a rank two isotropic sublattice be doubly admissible.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
L
be a nondegenerate primitive sublattice of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
and let
I
be a doubly admissible rank two isotropic sublattice of
L^{\perp}
. The following are equivalent:

(1)

I
is doubly admissible in
L^{\perp}
.

(2)

The primitive embedding
I\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
factors through primitive embeddings
I\hookrightarrow H\oplus H\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
.

(3)

The primitive embedding
L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}
factors through primitive embeddings
L\hookrightarrow H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}
.

Proof.

(1)\Rightarrow(2)
It follows from the definition that
e_{1}
is
1
-admissible in
L^{\perp}
. So, by
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
, we have a primitive embedding
i\colon H\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
such that
e_{1}
is one of the generators of
i(H)
, and we may decompose
L^{\perp}=i(H)\oplus(i(H))^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}
. Now define
g_{2}^{\prime}=g_{2}-\langle g_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}e_{1}
.
Then it is easy to show that
g_{2}^{\prime}\in(i(H))^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}
is isotropic with
\langle e_{2},g_{2}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=1
, so
e_{2}
is
1
-admissible in
(i(H))^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}
. We may therefore apply
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
again to obtain a primitive embedding
j\colon H\hookrightarrow(i(H))^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}
, where
e_{2}
is one of the generators of
j(H)
. Putting everything together gives the required factorisation of
I\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
.

(2)\Rightarrow(1)
Let
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
denote a set of primitive generators for
I\subset H\oplus H
. By
[
Sca87
, Lemma 4.1.2]
, primitive isotropic vectors in
H\oplus H
are unique up to automorphism, so we have
e_{1}^{\perp}/e_{1}\cong H
(where the orthogonal complement is taken in
H\oplus H
). The vector
e_{2}
gives rise to a primitive isotropic vector in
e_{1}^{\perp}/e_{1}
, so can be taken to be one of the standard generators for
H
. Let
g_{2}\in e_{1}^{\perp}
be a vector that gives rise to the other standard generator of
H
; then by construction
g_{2}
satisfies
\langle e_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=0
,
\langle e_{2},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=1
, and
\langle g_{2},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=0
.

Now, since
e_{1}
is primitive, there exists
f_{1}\in H\oplus H
such that
\langle e_{1},f_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=1
. Define

g_{1}=f_{1}-\langle f_{1},e_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}g_{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f_{1},f_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}e_{1}+\langle f_{1},e_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}\langle f_{1},g_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}e_{1},

which is well-defined as
H\oplus H
is an even lattice. Then
g_{1}
satisfies
\langle e_{1},g_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=1
,
\langle e_{2},g_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=0
, and
\langle g_{1},g_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{K}3}=0
, so the classes
\{e_{1},e_{2},g_{1},g_{2}\}
satisfy the conditions of Definition
3.31
.

(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
By
[
Nik80
, Proposition 1.6.1]
, up to isometry there are unique primitive embeddings
H\oplus H\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
and
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
. Therefore the orthogonal complements
(H\oplus H)^{\perp}_{\Lambda_{\text{K3}}}
and
(H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8})^{\perp}_{\Lambda_{\text{K3}}}
do not depend upon the choice of embedding, and are always isomorphic to
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
and
H\oplus H
respectively. Given this, equivalence of (2) and (3) is obtained by taking orthogonal complements.
∎

The next proposition gives some consequences of the doubly admissible condition.

Proposition 3.33
.

Let
L
be a nondegenerate primitive sublattice of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
and let
I
be a doubly admissible rank two isotropic sublattice of
L^{\perp}
. Then:

(1)

The composition
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}\to I^{\perp}/I
of the primitive embedding from Lemma
3.32
(3) and the quotient map is an isomorphism. In particular, the primitive embedding
L\hookrightarrow H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
from Lemma
3.32
(3) induces a primitive embedding

L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}/I
.

(2)

There is an orthogonal decomposition
L^{\perp}\cong H\oplus H\oplus\Gamma
, where the embedding
H\oplus H\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
is the one from Lemma
3.32
and
\Gamma
is a negative definite lattice.

In particular, if
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a doubly admissible
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, then
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is primitively
L
-polarised..

Proof.

(1)

This follows from the proof of
(2)\Leftrightarrow(3)
in Lemma
3.32
. It follows from that argument that we may decompose
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}=(H\oplus H)\oplus(H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8})
, with
I
embedding primitively into the first factor
H\oplus H
and
L
embedding primitively into the second factor
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. The quotient
I^{\perp}/I
is then isomorphic to the second factor
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
and the result follows.

(2)

Follows from
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
and the proof of
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
in Lemma
3.32
.

∎

3.7.
Moduli spaces

As in the previous sections, we let
L\subset\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
be a non-degenerate primitive sublattice of the K3 lattice of signature
(1,\rank(L)-1)
and fix a vector
h\in L\otimes\mathbb{R}
such that
\langle h,h\rangle_{L}>0
and
h\notin L^{\prime}\otimes\mathbb{R}
for any proper sublattice
L^{\prime}\subsetneq L
. Following
[
AE23
, Section 2.3]
, we define a moduli space of
L
-polarised K3 surfaces as follows.

Definition 3.34
.

[
AE23
, Definition 2.10]
An
L
-polarised K3 surface
is a surface
\overline{X}
with at worst du Val (ADE) singularities, whose minimal resolution is a smooth K3 surface, together with an isometric embedding
j\colon L\hookrightarrow\Pic(\overline{X})
for which
j(h)
is ample.

Note that one may obtain an
L
-polarised K3 surface
\overline{X}
from an
L
-quasipolarised K3 surface
X
by contracting all
(-2)
-curves in
j(L)^{\perp}\subset\NS(X)
.

Now define the period domain for
L
-polarised K3 surfaces to be

\mathcal{D}_{L}:=\mathbb{P}\{v\in\Lambda_{\text{K3}}\otimes\mathbb{C}\mid\langle v,v\rangle=0,\ \langle v,\overline{v}\rangle>0,\ \langle v,L\rangle=0\}

and define
\Gamma_{L}
to be the group of isometries of
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
that fix
L
,

\Gamma_{L}:=\{\phi\in O(\Lambda_{\text{K3}})\mid\phi(v)=v\text{ for any }v\in L\}.

By
[
Dol96
, Proposition 3.3]
, there is a natural injective homomorphism
\Gamma_{L}\hookrightarrow O(L^{\perp})
which identifies
\Gamma_{L}
with the subgroup

\widetilde{O}(L^{\perp}):=\ker\{O(L^{\perp})\to O(\disc(L^{\perp}))\},

where
O(\disc(L^{\perp}))
is the group of automorphisms of
\disc(L^{\perp})
that preserve the quadratic form. With this setup, the coarse moduli space of
L
-polarised K3 surfaces is given by the quotient
\mathcal{D}_{L}/\Gamma_{L}
.

This moduli space admits a Baily-Borel compactification. Boundary components in this compactification correspond to primitive isotropic sublattices of
L^{\perp}
of rank
1
(
0
-cusps) or
2
(
1
-cusps), up to the action of
\Gamma_{L}\cong\widetilde{O}(L^{\perp})
(see
[
AE23
, Definition 5.5]
). The following proposition shows how this fits with our existing theory.

Proposition 3.35
.

Each
1
-cusp in the Baily-Borel compactification of
\mathcal{D}_{L}/\Gamma_{L}
uniquely determines an embedding of
L
into
I^{\perp}/I
, up to the action of
O(I^{\perp}/I)
.

Proof.

By the discussion above,
1
-cusps in the Baily-Borel compactification are determined by first fixing an embedding
L\subset\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
, up to the action of
O(\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3})
, then choosing a rank
2
isotropic sublattice
I\subset L^{\perp}
, up to the action of
\Gamma_{L}
. It is immediate from the definitions that this is equivalent to choosing a pair
(L,I)
of sublattices of
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
, up to the action of
O(\Lambda_{\text{K3}})
, such that
I
is isotropic of rank
2
and orthogonal to
L
.

Now we reverse the order in which we choose
L
and
I
. Begin by fixing a rank
2
isotropic sublattice
I
of
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
; by
[
Sca87
, Section 2.2]
this choice is unique up to
O(\Lambda_{\text{K3}})
. It follows from the discussion above that
1
-cusps in the Baily-Borel compactification then correspond to embeddings
L\subset I^{\perp}
, up to the action of

\Stab(I):=\{\phi\in O(\Lambda_{\text{K3}})\mid\phi(v)=v\text{ for any }v\in I\}.

As
L\subset I^{\perp}
is non-degenerate we have
L\cap I=\{0\}
, so
L
descends to a sublattice of
I^{\perp}/I
. Moreover, any element of
\Stab(I)
fixes
I
and acts as an isometry on
I^{\perp}
, so descends to an element of
O(I^{\perp}/I)
. We thus see that a choice of embedding
L\subset I^{\perp}
, up to the action of
\Stab(I)
, uniquely determines an embedding of
L
into
I^{\perp}/I
, up to the action of
O(I^{\perp}/I)
.
∎

Remark 3.36
.

The correspondence from Proposition
3.35
is not one-to-one: an embedding of
L
into
I^{\perp}/I
, up to
O(I^{\perp}/I)
, does not necessarily uniquely determine a
1
-cusp in the Baily-Borel compactification; see
[
Sca87
, Section 2.2]
.

In the Baily-Borel compactification of
\mathcal{D}_{L}/\Gamma_{L}
, Type II degenerations occur over the
1
-cusps and Type III degenerations over the
0
-cusps. For an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, the embedding of
L
into
I^{\perp}/I
appearing in Proposition
3.35
is precisely the one from Lemma
3.22
.

In the reminder of this section we study the consequences of the doubly admissible condition for the structure of the Baily-Borel compactification. We begin with a lemma.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
I,I^{\prime}\subset H\oplus H
be two primitive isotropic sublattices of rank
2
and let
e\in I
,
e^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
be primitive isotropic vectors. Then there exists an isometry
g
of
H\oplus H
such that
g(I)=I^{\prime}
and
g(e)=e^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
e\in I\subset H\oplus H
be as in the lemma and let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},,e_{4}
be a standard basis for
H\oplus H
. To prove the lemma, it suffices to show that there is an isometry
g
of
H\oplus H
such that
g(e)=e_{1}
and
g(I)
is the isotropic sublattice of rank
2
generated by
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
.

Indeed, by
[
Sca87
, Lemma 4.1.2]
, there exists an isometry
g
of
H\oplus H
with
g(e)=e_{1}
. Let
f\in I
be a primitive element such that
e
and
f
generate
I
. Write
g(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{4}a_{i}e_{i}
. Then
\langle g(f),g(e)\rangle_{H\oplus H}=\langle g(f),e_{1}\rangle_{H\oplus H}=a_{2}=0
and as
\langle g(f),g(f)\rangle_{H\oplus H}=a_{3}a_{4}=0
we have
a_{3}=0
or
a_{4}=0
. By applying an isometry swapping
e_{3}
and
e_{4}
, if necessary, we may therefore assume that
g(f)=a_{1}e_{1}+a_{3}e_{3}
. So
g(f-a_{1}e)=a_{3}e_{3}
and, by primitivity of
g(I)
, we must have that
g(I)
is the sublattice generated by
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
, as required.
∎

Proposition 3.38
.

Suppose that
I
is doubly admissible in
L^{\perp}
. Then the
1
-cusp corresponding to
I
in the Baily-Borel compactification of
\mathcal{D}_{L}/\Gamma_{L}
is incident to a unique
0
-cusp.

Proof.

Let
e,e^{\prime}\in I
be any two primitive isotropic vectors. To show that the
1
-cusp corresponding to
I
is incident to a unique
0
-cusp, it suffices to show that there is an element of
\Gamma_{L}\cong\widetilde{O}(L^{\perp})
taking
e
to
e^{\prime}
.

By Proposition
3.33
, we have a decomposition
L^{\perp}=H\oplus H\oplus\Gamma
, where
I
embeds primitively into the
H\oplus H
factor. By Lemma
3.37
, there exists an isometry
g
of
H\oplus H
with
g(I)=I
and
g(e)=e^{\prime}
. We can extend
g
to an isometry
\hat{g}
of
L^{\perp}
by the identity on
\Gamma
. As
\disc(L^{\perp})=\disc(\Gamma)
and
\hat{g}
acts as the identity on
\Gamma
, it follows that
\hat{g}\in\widetilde{O}(L^{\perp})
, as required.
∎

Our final result gives an explicit description of the
1
-cusps in the Baily-Borel compactification which correspond to doubly admissible sublattices
I\subset L
.

Proposition 3.39
.

There is a bijection between
1
-cusps in the Baily-Borel compactification of
\mathcal{D}_{L}/\Gamma_{L}
corresponding to doubly admissible
I\subset L^{\perp}
and negative definite lattices
\Gamma
of rank
(18-\rank(L))
with
\disc(\Gamma)\cong\disc(L^{\perp})
.

Proof.

From Lemmas
3.32
and
3.37
and the proof of Proposition
3.38
, it follows that
1
-cusps in the Baily-Borel compactification corresponding to doubly admissible
I\subset L^{\perp}
are in bijection with embeddings of
H\oplus H\hookrightarrow L^{\perp}
up to the action of
\widetilde{O}(L^{\perp})
. But by
[
Nik80
, Proposition 1.5.1]
, such embeddings are in bijection with negative definite lattices
\Gamma
of rank
(18-\rank(L))
with
\disc(\Gamma)\cong\disc(L^{\perp})
; here
\Gamma
is the orthogonal complement of
H\oplus H
inside
L^{\perp}
.
∎

4.
Elliptic Fibrations on K3 Surfaces

4.1.
Pseudolattices from elliptic fibrations over discs

Before discussing the setting of elliptic fibrations on K3 surfaces, we begin with a simpler setting. The definitions in this section broadly follow those in
[
HT20
, Section 4]
, although we note that we do not work in precisely the same setting: in particular,
[
HT20
, Section 4]
assumes that all singular fibres have Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
.

Let
\Delta:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|\leq 1\}
denote the closed complex unit disc and let
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
denote a proper surjective holomorphic map from an oriented nonsingular compact complex surface (with boundary)
W
to
\Delta
whose general fibre is a nonsingular elliptic curve. Assume that
\pi_{W}
is relatively minimal and has no multiple fibres. Let
\Sigma_{W}\subset\Delta
denote the set of points over which the fibres of
W
are singular; we assume that
\Sigma_{W}
is finite and contained in the interior of
\Delta
, so there are no singular fibres over points on the boundary. We call such
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
satisfying these assumptions
elliptic fibrations over discs
.

Let
p\in\partial\Delta
denote a point on the boundary of
\Delta
and let
F=\pi^{-1}_{W}(p)
denote the fibre over
p
. We consider the relative homology
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
. There is a natural map
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
coming from the long exact sequence of a pair; we call this map the
asymptotic charge
. Recall from Section
2.1
that
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
, equipped with the usual intersection form, can be identified with the elliptic curve pseudolattice
\mathrm{E}
.

We may define a bilinear form, called the
Seifert pairing
, on
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
. Choose
\varepsilon>0
so that
\Sigma\subset\Delta_{\varepsilon}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|\leq 1-\varepsilon\}
(i.e. there are no singular fibres in an
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
\partial\Delta
). Fix a group of diffeomorphisms
\varphi_{\theta}\colon\Delta\to\Delta
, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, such that
\varphi
fixes the disc
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
and acts as a clockwise rotation through an angle of
\theta
on
\partial\Delta
. Let
\psi_{\theta}
denote a family of lifts of these diffeomorphisms to diffeomorphisms of
W
.

Definition 4.1
.

(c.f.
[
HT20
, Definition 4.2]
) The
Seifert pairing

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\times H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Z}

is the map sending
(v,w)
to the negative of the topological intersection product
-\langle v,(\psi_{\pi})_{*}w\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

We note that there is a sign error in
[
HT20
, Definition 4.2]
; the negative sign on the topological intersection form is missing. This does not affect the remaining results of
[
HT20
]
; in particular, the proof of
[
HT20
, Theorem 4.14]
uses the correct sign convention, in which the Seifert pairing has the opposite sign to the bilinear form defined by Auroux in the proof of
[
Aur05
, Lemma 16]
.

The first main result of this section is the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
be an elliptic fibration over a disc. Then with notation as above:

(1)

H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
, equipped with the Seifert pairing, is a unimodular pseudolattice.

(2)

The Serre operator on
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
is given by
(\psi_{-2\pi})_{*}
.

(3)

The asymptotic charge map
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is a relative
(-1)^{0}
-Calabi-Yau spherical homomorphism.

(4)

The twist map
T_{\phi}\colon H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is the action of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
.

Remark 4.4
.

With the additional assumption that all of the singular fibres of
\pi_{W}
are of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
, this result follows immediately from
[
HT20
, Proposition 4.4]
and
[
HT20
, Corollary 4.5]
.

Proof.

The main proof proceeds in two steps, which we have separated out as lemmas. In Lemma
4.5
, we show that
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
can be decomposed into a set of simpler pieces, and that if (1), (3), and (4) hold for the pieces, then they also hold for
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
. Then, in Lemma
4.7
, we prove that (1), (3), and (4) hold for the pieces of the decomposition. Combining these results proves statements (1), (3), and (4).

Finally, the proof of (2) follows by the same argument that was used to prove the corresponding statement in
[
HT20
, Proposition 4.4]
. Indeed, for any
u_{1},u_{2}\in H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\langle u_{2},(\psi_{-2\pi})_{*}u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=-\langle u_{2},(\psi_{-\pi})_{*}u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=-\langle(\psi_{\pi})_{*}u_{2},u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}},

where the second equality holds as
\psi_{\pi}
is a diffeomorphism. Finally, the topological intersection product is symmetric on
4
-manifolds, so
\langle u_{2},(\psi_{-2\pi})_{*}u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=-\langle u_{1},(\psi_{\pi})_{*}u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}
, as required.
∎

Before stating the first lemma, we set up some more notation. Label the points in
\Sigma
as
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
as follows. Connect
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}
to
p
by straight lines in
\Delta
, choosing labels so that these lines emanate from
p
in a clockwise order; if
m\geq 2
points lie on the same line through
p
, label them
p_{i},p_{i+1},\ldots,p_{i+m-1}
in order of increasing distance from
p
. Label the fibre
\pi_{W}^{-1}(p_{i})
over
p_{i}
by
F_{i}
.

Lemma 4.5
.

There are elliptic fibrations over discs
W_{i}\to\Delta_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, each containing precisely one singular fibre of the same Kodaira type as
F_{i}
, so that

H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}H_{2}(W_{i},F;\mathbb{Z}).

Moreover, if all of the
H_{2}(W_{i},F;\mathbb{Z})
satisfy (1), (3), and (4) from Proposition
4.3
, then so does
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
, and we have a decomposition of pseudolattices

H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\cdots\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{k},

where
\mathrm{G}_{i}:=H_{2}(W_{i},F;\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{E}:=H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 4.6
.

When all of the singular fibres in
W
have Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
, this result is essentially
[
HT20
, Proposition 4.4(3)]
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the result holds for a decomposition into
W_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
and
W_{2}\to\Delta_{2}
, where
W_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
contains precisely one singular fibre of the same Kodaira type as
F_{1}
and
W_{2}\to\Delta_{2}
contains all other singular fibres; the general result then follows by induction.

Let
\gamma
be a line through
p
that passes between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
and divides
\Delta
into two parts; if
p
,
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
all lie on the same line, deform this line to pass slightly to the right of
p_{1}
and slightly to the left of
p_{2}
, as viewed from
p
. By construction,
\gamma
divides
\Delta
into two parts, with
p_{1}
on the left and
p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}
on the right, as viewed from
p
. Label the part containing
p_{i}
by
\Delta_{i}^{\circ}
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Let
\gamma_{\varepsilon}\subset\Delta
denote a closed
\varepsilon
-neighbourhood of
\gamma
, where
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough so that
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
does not contain any of the
p_{i}
. Finally, define
\Delta_{i}=\Delta_{i}^{\circ}\cup\gamma_{\varepsilon}
and
W_{i}=\pi_{W}^{-1}(\Delta_{i})
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Note that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\Delta_{i}
is homeomorphic to a closed disc and
p\in\partial\Delta_{i}
, so
W_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
(resp.
W_{2}\to\Delta_{2}
) is an elliptic fibration over a disc containing
F_{1}
(resp.
F_{2},\ldots,F_{p}
) and
F\subset W_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.

With this setup, noting that
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
is simply connected and the restriction of the fibration
\pi_{W}
to
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
is trivial, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle H_{2}(W_{1},F;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(W_{2},F;\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\iota_{1})_{*}(\alpha)+(\iota_{2})_{*}(\beta),

where
\iota_{j}\colon W_{j}\to W
denotes the inclusion.

Given this, the remainder of the proposition will follow from general properties of the pseudolattice sum (Definition
2.11
) if we can show that
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
where
\mathrm{G}_{i}:=H_{2}(W_{i},F;\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{E}:=H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
. But this is a straightforward consequence of the construction: the asymptotic charge
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is the sum
\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}
of the asymptotic charges for
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
, and if
(u_{1},u_{2})\in H_{2}(W_{1},F;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(W_{2},F;\mathbb{Z})
then the Seifert pairing on
W
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}\langle u_{1},u_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}&\langle\phi_{1}(u_{1}),\phi_{2}(u_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}\\
0&\langle u_{2},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}\end{pmatrix},

which is precisely the definition of the bilinear form on
\mathrm{G}_{1}\oright_{\mathrm{E}}\mathrm{G}_{2}
.
∎

This lemma reduces the proof of Proposition
4.3
to the case of a fibration containing precisely one singular fibre. This setting is dealt with by our second lemma.

Lemma 4.7
.

Suppose that
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
is an elliptic fibration over a disc containing precisely one singular fibre. Then
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
, equipped with the Seifert pairing, is a unimodular pseudolattice. Moreover, for some choice of basis
(a,b)
for
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{E}
, we have an isomorphism between
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})\to\mathrm{E}
(see Example
2.13
), for
v_{i}\in\mathrm{E}
depending upon the Kodaira type of the singular fibre as given in Table
4.1
. In particular, the asymptotic charge map
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is a relative
(-1)^{0}
-Calabi-Yau spherical homomorphism. Finally, the twist map
T_{\phi}\colon H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is the action of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
.

Kodaira type

H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})

\mathrm{I}_{n}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,\ldots,a)
with
n
copies of
a

\mathrm{I}_{n}^{*}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,\ldots,a,b-a,b+a)
with
(n+4)
copies of
a

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}

\mathrm{Z}(a,b+a)

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,b+a)

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{V}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,b+a)

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{V}^{*}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a,b+a)

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}^{*}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a,b+a)

\mathrm{I}\mathrm{I}^{*}

\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a,b+a)

Table 4.1
.
Pseudolattices
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
for
W
an elliptic fibration over a disc containing one singular fibre.

Proof.

This proof is a lengthy explicit case analysis. Essentially, for each type of singular fibre our approach is to use geometric considerations to construct a basis for
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and to compute the Seifert pairing for it, then to use a computer algebra system to solve the resulting linear algebra problem and find the required isomorphism. We present a representative sample of cases here to illustrate the method; the other cases are analogous.

We begin with some general setup. Let
p\in\partial\Delta
denote a point on the boundary of
\Delta
, let
F=\pi_{W}^{-1}(p)
, and let
q\in\Delta
be the point over which the singular fibre occurs. As
\pi_{W}
has only one singular fibre, which is not multiple, we may assume that
\pi_{W}
admits a section
\sigma
over
\Delta
. Let
\overline{\pi}_{W}\colon\overline{W}\to\Delta
denote the quotient of
W
by the fibrewise elliptic involution defined by this section; note that the double cover
W\to\overline{W}
is ramified along
\sigma
and a trisection of
\pi_{W}
.

The simplest case is
\mathrm{I}_{n}
. Choose a path
\alpha
from
p
to
q
. The ramification locus of
W\to\overline{W}
contains two disjoint sections,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, which meet the same component of the
\mathrm{I}_{n}
fibre.
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
intersect
\overline{\pi}_{W}^{-1}(\alpha)
in a pair of curves; take a strip in
\overline{\pi}_{W}^{-1}(\alpha)
joining them (see Figure
1(a)
). The double cover of this strip under
W\to\overline{W}
is a thimble
t_{\alpha}
in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
; let
a
denote the corresponding vanishing cycle. Consider the set of classes in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
given by
t_{\alpha}
along with the components
e_{1},\ldots,e_{n-1}
of the
\mathrm{I}_{n}
fibre which do not meet
\sigma
. One may check that these classes generate
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and the Seifert pairing
\chi
and asymptotic charge map
\phi
are given by

, it follows that the Seifert pairing is unimodular and these classes form a basis for

.

5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle t_{\beta}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle-1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]$}}\quad\phi=\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep&&
\kern\arraycolsep\hfil$\@kbrowstyle#$\ifkbalignright\relax\else\hfil\fi\kern\arraycolsep\cr 5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e_{0}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e_{1}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e_{2}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle e_{4}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle t_{\alpha}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle t_{\beta}$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle a$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\\
5.0pt\hfil\hbox{\vrule height=0.0pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.0pt}$\scriptstyle b$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 0$\hfil\kern 5.0pt&5.0pt\hfil$\scriptstyle 1$\hfil\kern 5.0pt\crcr}}}}\right]$}}.

Linear algebra can be used to verify that there is a change of basis in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
which takes
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
to
\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a)\to\mathrm{E}
and that the twist map is the usual action
\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}
of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
.

The most complicated case that we will cover here is
\mathrm{III}^{*}
; the other cases are no more difficult. In this case we again choose two paths
\alpha
and
\beta
from
p
to
q
, intersecting only at
p
and
q
, with
\alpha
passing to the left of
\beta
as viewed from
p
. The ramification locus of
W\to\overline{W}
contains two disjoint sections,
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, along with a bisection. These sections intersect
\overline{\pi}^{-1}_{W}(\alpha)
and
\overline{\pi}^{-1}_{W}(\beta)
in two disjoint sections and a bisection ramified over
q
. Take a strip in
\overline{\pi}^{-1}_{W}(\alpha)
joining
\sigma_{1}
to one branch of the bisection and a strip in
\overline{\pi}^{-1}_{W}(\beta)
joining
\sigma_{2}
to the other branch (see Figure
1(c)
). The double cover of these strips under
W\to\overline{W}
are thimbles
t_{\alpha}
and
t_{\beta}
in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
; let
a
and

denote the corresponding vanishing cycles. Label the components of the
\mathrm{I}_{0}^{*}
fibre such that
e_{1},\ldots,e_{7}
form the chain joining
\sigma_{1}
to
\sigma_{2}
and
e_{0}
is the component meeting the bisection. Consider the set of classes in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
given by
t_{\alpha}
and
t_{\beta}
along with the components

of the
\mathrm{I}_{0}
fibre which do not meet

. One may check that these classes generate
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and the Seifert pairing

\phi=\hbox{}\vbox{\kern 0.86108pt\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\kern 2.5pt\kern-5.0pt\left[\kern 0.0pt\kern-2.5pt\kern-5.55557pt\vbox{\kern-0.86108pt\vbox{\vbox{\halign{\kern\arraycolsep\hfil\@arstrut$\kbcolstyle#$\hfil\kern\arraycolsep&
