Linear algebra can then be used to verify that the twist map is the usual action
\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}
of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
and that there are changes of bases
2
2

2

It is important to note in the
\mathrm{III}^{*}
case that the basis
(a,b)\in\mathrm{E}
defining the pseudolattice
\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a,b+a)
is
not
the same as the basis
(a,b)
of
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
constructed here (the twist maps do not match), but the two are related by a change of basis in
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{E}
.
in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
which take
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
to
\mathrm{Z}(a,a,a,a,a,a,b-a,b+a,b+a)\to\mathrm{E}
.
∎

The following result is a consequence of Lemma
4.7
; whilst we will not need it in the sequel, we present it here out of interest.

Proposition 4.8
.

Suppose that
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
is an elliptic fibration over a disc containing precisely one singular fibre. Then there exists a deformation of
\pi_{W}
to a genus
1
Lefschetz fibration
\tilde{\pi}_{W}\colon\tilde{W}\to\Delta
(i.e. an elliptic fibration over a disc in which all singular fibres are of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{1}
), such that
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{2}(\tilde{W},\tilde{F};\mathbb{Z})
are isomorphic as pseudolattices.

Proof.

For each Kodaira fibre type, the existence of an appropriate deformation to a genus
1
Lefschetz fibration
\tilde{\pi}_{W}\colon\tilde{W}\to\Delta
is given by
[
GHS16
, Theorem 3.1]
and, for such deformations, explicit bases of thimbles for
H_{2}(\tilde{W},\tilde{F};\mathbb{Z})
have been computed in the string junction literature (see, for example,
[
FYY00
, Section 3.1]
). From this, the pseudolattice structure on
H_{2}(\tilde{W},\tilde{F};\mathbb{Z})
can be immediately deduced from
[
HT20
, Proposition 4.4]
. The pseudolattices thus obtained are precisely those given in Table
4.1
3
3

3

Caution: the reader attempting to replicate the computation in this proof should take careful note of conventions for signs, orientations, and compositions, which are not consistent between the three papers
[
FYY00
,
GHS16
,
HT20
]
!
.
∎

To conclude this section, we discuss when
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism. The following assumption will be critical.

Assumption 4.9
.

There exists a symplectic basis
(a,b)
for
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
such that:

(1)

the action of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}1&e(W)-12\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

where
e(W)
denotes the topological Euler number of
W
; and

(2)

r(a)
is primitive in
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
, where
r
is the right adjoint to the asymptotic charge map
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\pi_{W}\colon W\to\Delta
be an elliptic fibration over a disc and assume that there exists a symplectic basis
(a,b)
for
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
so that Assumption
4.9
holds. Then
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism.

Proof.

We check the conditions from the definition. Condition (1) follows immediately from
[
HT20
, Proposition 3.1]
and condition (2) from Proposition
4.3
. Condition (3) follows from Lemmas
4.5
and
4.7
, which together give an isomorphism between
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{Z}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r})
, for some
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
.

Condition (4) is the most difficult to check. The proof of
[
HT20
, Theorem 4.14]
, which occupies most of
[
HT20
, Section 4.3]
, applies verbatim in our setting, as long as one notes the following two facts.

•

Auroux’s result from
[
Aur05
]
, stated as
[
HT20
, Lemma 4.8]
, still holds in our more general setting. Indeed, if
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are classes in the kernel of the asymptotic charge map
\phi
then, by the long exact sequence of a pair,
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
are classes from
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
with no boundary in
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
, so we have
\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=-\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}
. It follows that the signature of the Seifert pairing on the kernel of
\phi
is the negative of the signature of the topological intersection form on
H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
.

•

Matsumoto’s result
[
Mat83
, Corollary 8.1]
on “fractional signatures” of singular fibres holds for fibres
F_{i}
of any Kodaira type, giving
\phi(\tau_{i})+\sigma(N_{i})=-\frac{2}{3}\chi(F_{i})
where
\chi(F_{i})
is the Euler number of the fibre.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.10
. ∎

4.2.
Elliptically fibred K3 surfaces

Now we shift our attention to K3 surfaces. Let
Y
be a K3 surface which admits an elliptic fibration
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. We do not assume that
\pi
has a section but we note that, by
[
Huy16
, Proposition 11.1.6]
,
\pi
cannot have multiple fibres. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
denote the finite set of points over which the fibres of
\pi
are singular.

Let
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\Sigma
be a simple loop and let
p,q\in\gamma
be two distinct points. Let
F_{p}=\pi^{-1}(p)
and
F_{q}=\pi^{-1}(q)
denote the fibres over
p
and
q
. Finally, let
U
denote the open set
U:=Y\setminus F_{q}
.

\gamma
divides
\mathbb{P}^{1}
into two pieces which are homeomorphic to discs
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
. Let
\pi_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
and
\pi_{2}\colon Y_{2}\to\Delta_{2}
denote the induced elliptic fibrations over discs. Then Proposition
4.3
holds for the asymptotic charge maps
\phi_{i}\colon H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Definition 4.11
.

A simple loop
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\Sigma
is called
allowable
if Assumption
4.9
holds for both
\phi_{1}\colon H_{2}(Y_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
and
\phi_{2}\colon H_{2}(Y_{2},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 4.12
.

We note that if Assumption
4.9
(1) holds for one of
\pi_{1}\colon Y_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
or
\pi_{2}\colon Y_{2}\to\Delta_{2}
, then it automatically holds for both, as the anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta_{1}
is the inverse of the anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta_{2}
and
e(Y_{1})+e(Y_{2})=e(Y)=24
.

Assuming that
\gamma
is allowable, Theorem
4.10
implies that the maps
\phi_{i}\colon H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, are quasi del Pezzo homomorphisms of pseudolattices. Moreover, by Proposition
2.14
, if we denote the canonical class for each
i\in\{1,2\}
by
K_{i}\in\NS(H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z}))
, then we have

q(K_{1},K_{1})=12-e(Y_{1})=e(Y_{2})-12=-q(K_{2},K_{2}),

since
e(Y_{1})+e(Y_{2})=24
. We are thus in the setting of Section
2.3
; as in that setting, we will assume labels have been chosen so that
q(K_{1},K_{1})\geq 0
and consequently
e(Y_{1})\leq 12
.

Let
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
denote the spherical homomorphism obtained by gluing the pseudolattices
\mathrm{G}_{1}=H_{2}(Y_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{G}_{2}=H_{2}(Y_{2},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
along
\mathrm{E}\cong H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
via the map
\phi_{1}\oright(-\phi_{2})
, as in Section
2.3
, and let
r
be its right adjoint. Note that
\deg(\mathrm{G})=12-e(Y_{1})
. The results of Section
2.3
give rise to lattices
\overline{\mathrm{E}}
,
\mathrm{K}
, and
\mathrm{M}
, related by the short exact sequence (
2
).

Lemma 4.13
.

\mathrm{G}
is naturally isomorphic to
H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
(as
\mathbb{Z}
-modules) and the spherical homomorphism
f\colon\mathrm{G}\to\mathrm{E}
coincides with the boundary
H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
from the long exact sequence of a pair.

Under Poincaré duality, the lattice
\mathrm{K}
is isomorphic to
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
(as
\mathbb{Z}
-modules), where
F
denotes the class of a fibre in
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 4.14
.

From the viewpoint of the perverse Leray filtration
P_{\bullet}
on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Q})
, as described in
[
DT21
, Sections 2 and 5.1.1]
, the class
F
generates
P_{1}H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Q})
, and the quotient
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Q})/\mathbb{Q}F
may be identified with the graded piece
\Gr^{P}_{1}H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Q})
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same idea as the proof of Lemma
4.5
, with a crucial difference (see Remark
4.15
).

Begin by choosing
\varepsilon>0
so that the open ball
B_{\varepsilon}(q)\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
does not contain
p
or any point of
\Sigma
. Then define
\overline{U}:=Y\setminus\pi^{-1}B_{\varepsilon}(q)
. Note that
\overline{U}
is a closed manifold with boundary and is a deformation retract of
U
, so
H_{2}(\overline{U},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
and
H_{2}(\overline{U};\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})\cong H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
, where the last isomorphism is Poincaré duality.

Now let
\gamma_{\delta}
denote a closed
\delta
-neighbourhood of
\gamma
inside
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(q)
, where
0<\delta<\varepsilon
is chosen small enough that
\gamma_{\delta}
does not contain any point of
\Sigma
. Then define
\overline{\Delta_{i}}:=(\Delta_{i}\cup\gamma_{\delta})\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(q)
and
\overline{Y}_{i}:=\pi^{-1}(\overline{\Delta}_{i})
. Note that, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\overline{\Delta}_{i}
is homeomorphic to a closed disc and
\overline{Y}_{i}
is homeomorphic to
Y_{i}
. Note further that
\overline{Y}_{1}
and
\overline{Y}_{2}
cover
\overline{U}
, with
\overline{Y}_{1}\cap\overline{Y}_{2}=\pi^{-1}(\gamma_{\delta})

With this setup, noting that
\gamma_{\delta}
is simply connected and the restriction of the fibration
\pi
to
\gamma_{\delta}
is trivial, the Mayer-Vietoris sequence gives an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathrm{G}\cong H_{2}(\overline{Y}_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(\overline{Y}_{2},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H_{2}(\overline{U},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\iota_{1})_{*}(\alpha)-(\iota_{2})_{*}(\beta),

where
\iota_{j}\colon\overline{Y}_{j}\to\overline{U}
denotes the inclusion. Moreover, by construction the boundary map is given by
\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}
, where
\phi_{i}\colon H_{2}(Y_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
are the asymptotic charge maps. This proves the first part of the statement.

For the second part, note that the long exact sequence of a pair gives

0\longrightarrow H_{2}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z}){\longrightarrow}H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow 0.

By definition,
\mathrm{K}
is the kernel of the boundary map
\phi
. By exactness, this is isomorphic to the quotient of
H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})
by the image of
H_{2}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, which is just the class of the fibre
F_{p}
. An application of Poincaré duality gives the result.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

We note that this proof differs from the proof of Lemma
4.5
in the choice of sign in the isomorphism coming from the Mayer-Vietoris sequence. This corresponds to a choice of orientation on the boundaries of the two pieces, which affects how they glue: in Lemma
4.5
, we glue with the same orientation, whereas in the lemma above we glue with opposite orientations.

Definition 4.16
.

We may place a bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
as follows. As in the proof of Lemma
4.13
, choose
\varepsilon>0
so that the open ball
B_{\varepsilon}(q)\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
does not contain any point of
\Sigma
. Then define
\overline{U}:=Y\setminus\pi^{-1}B_{\varepsilon}(q)
.

\overline{U}
is a closed manifold with boundary and is a deformation retract of
U
. We therefore have isomorphisms
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(\overline{U};\mathbb{Z})
, where the first isomorphism is Poincaré duality. As a manifold with boundary,
H_{2}(\overline{U};\mathbb{Z})
has a topological intersection product, which defines a symmetric bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
by the isomorphism above.

The class
F
is totally degenerate with respect to this bilinear form, so this form descends to the quotient
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
.

Lemma 4.17
.

Under the isomorphism of Lemma
4.13
, the bilinear form induced on
\mathrm{K}
from
\mathrm{G}
coincides with the negative of the bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
induced by Definition
4.16
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{K}
be any two elements. Expressing
\mathrm{K}
as a sublattice of
\mathrm{G}
, by the proof of Lemma
4.13
we may decompose
\alpha=(\iota_{1})_{*}(\alpha_{1})-(\iota_{2})_{*}(\alpha_{2})
and
\beta=(\iota_{1})_{*}(\beta_{1})-(\iota_{2})_{*}(\beta_{2})
for
\alpha_{j},\beta_{j}\in H_{2}(Y_{j},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
. Note that, as
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{K}=\ker(\phi_{1}\oright(-\phi_{2}))
, we have
\phi_{1}(\alpha_{1})=\phi_{2}(\alpha_{2})
and
\phi_{1}(\beta_{1})=\phi_{2}(\beta_{2})
. Now we compute

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}-\langle\phi_{1}(\alpha_{1}),\phi_{2}(\beta_{2})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}+\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}-\langle\phi_{1}(\alpha_{1}),\phi_{1}(\beta_{1})\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}+\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}

\displaystyle=\langle\beta_{1},\alpha_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}+\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}},

where the final equality comes from
[
HT20
, Lemma 2.21]
. Now, using the fact that the Serre operator on
H_{2}(Y_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
is given by
(\psi_{-2\pi})_{*}
(Proposition
4.3
), we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=\langle\beta_{1},\alpha_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}+\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}

\displaystyle=\langle\alpha_{1},(\psi_{-2\pi})_{*}\beta_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}+\langle\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\alpha_{1},(\psi_{-\pi})_{*}\beta_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}-\langle\alpha_{2},(\psi_{\pi})_{*}\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}.

Now, note that
\psi_{-\pi}
on
Y_{1}
and
\psi_{\pi}
on
Y_{2}
agree on the boundary
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}
, so
(\psi_{-\pi})_{*}
and
(\psi_{\pi})_{*}
are induced by a homeomorphism
\psi
on
Y
that “rotates the boundary”. Hence

\displaystyle\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\alpha_{1},(\psi_{-\pi})_{*}\beta_{1}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}-\langle\alpha_{2},(\psi_{\pi})_{*}\beta_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\alpha,\psi_{*}\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}

\displaystyle=-\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle_{\mathrm{top}},

where the topological intersection product involving
\alpha
and
\beta
is the one on
H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})
given by Definition
4.16
.
∎

This result allows us to identify the exact sequence (
2
) in this setting.

Proposition 4.18
.

There is a commutative diagram of
\mathbb{Z}
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathrm{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F}^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where the vertical arrows are isomorphisms and the orthogonal complement
F^{\perp}
is taken inside
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
.

Moreover, the symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{M}
are identified with the negatives of the bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
induced by Definition
4.16
and the form on
F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
induced by the cup product on
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, respectively.

Remark 4.19
.

From the viewpoint of the perverse Leray filtration
P_{\bullet}
on
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Q})
, from
[
DT21
, Proposition 2.16]
we have
F^{\perp}\cong P_{2}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Q})
and
F
is a generator of
P_{1}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Q})
, so
F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Q}F
is isomorphic to the graded piece
\Gr_{2}^{P}H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Q})
. Putting this together with Remark
4.14
, we see that the sequence in the proposition above appears as a perverse graded piece in the mirror Clemens-Schmid sequence; see
[
DT21
, Sections 3 and 5.1.1]
.

Proof.

The long exact sequence of cohomology with compact support in this setting reads

0\longrightarrow H^{1}(F_{q};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H^{2}(F_{q};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow 0

and the kernel of the map
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(F_{q};\mathbb{Z})
is
F^{\perp}
. Moreover, by construction, the bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
is compatible with the cup product on
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, as both are Poincaré dual to the topological intersection pairing.

Consequently, in light of the result of Lemma
4.17
, to prove the proposition it suffices to show that
\overline{\mathrm{E}}\subset{\mathrm{K}}
coincides with the image of
H^{1}(F_{q};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
under the isomorphism of Lemma
4.13
. By definition,
\overline{E}
is the saturation of the image of the map
r(v)=(r_{1}T_{-f_{2}}(v),-r_{2}(v))
, which gives the class
\beta\in H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
obtained by parallel transporting
v\in\mathrm{E}\cong H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
around
\partial\Delta_{1}
, then back along
\partial\Delta_{2}
in the opposite direction. The class
\beta
is homotopy equivalent to the class obtained by parallel transporting
v\in H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
around a small loop containing
q
. As monodromy around
q
acts trivially on
H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, the class
\beta
has no boundary, so lifts to
H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})
. But the set of all such classes is Poincaré dual to the image of
H^{1}(F_{q};\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

4.3.
Point-like vectors and the Néron-Severi lattice

Next we ask how the ideas of Section
2.4
arise in this setting.

Let
r_{i}\colon H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
denote the right adjoint of the asymptotic charge map
\phi_{i}
. As
\phi_{i}
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism (by Theorem
4.10
),
H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
is surface-like with point-like vector
r_{i}(a)
.

We can give a topological description of the class
r_{i}(a)
as follows. As the class
a\in H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
is monodromy invariant (by Assumption
4.9
), define
\alpha\in H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
to be the class of a torus obtained by parallel transporting
a
around
\partial\Delta_{i}
. Let
\beta\in H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
be any other element. Then, since
\alpha
is preserved under the diffeomorphism
(\psi_{\pi})_{*}
, we have

\langle\beta,-\alpha\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=\langle\beta,(\psi_{\pi})_{*}\alpha\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=\langle\beta,\alpha\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=\langle\phi_{i}(\beta),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}},

where the last equality follows because we can assume
\beta
and
\alpha
intersect only on
F_{p}
. Therefore, by uniqueness of the right adjoint, we have
r_{i}(a)=-\alpha
.

The Néron-Severi group of
\mathrm{G}_{i}
is given by

\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})=r_{i}(a)^{\perp}/r_{i}(a).

From the right adjoint property, for all
v\in H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
we have
\langle v,r_{i}(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{G}_{i}}=\langle\phi_{i}(v),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}
. Moreover, from the properties of the bilinear form on
\mathrm{E}\cong H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, we have
\langle\phi_{i}(v),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}
if and only if
\phi_{i}(v)
is a multiple of
a
. This gives an explicit description of the Néron-Severi group

\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})=\{v\in H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\mid\phi_{i}(v)\text{ is a multiple of }a\}/r_{1}(a).

Recall next that, from the proof of Lemma
4.13
, we have isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}
-modules

\mathrm{G}\cong H_{2}(U,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(Y_{1},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(Y_{2},F_{p};\mathbb{Z}).

In this description,
\Psi
can be taken to be the sublattice of
\mathrm{G}
generated by the classes of
\tau_{1}:=(-r_{1}(a),0)
and
\tau_{2}:=(0,-r_{2}(a))
. Under the isomorphism
\mathrm{K}\cong H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
, these generators are the classes of the tori in
U
obtained by parallel transporting
a
around the loop
\gamma
, passing to one side of the puncture
q
to obtain
\tau_{1}
and to the other side for
\tau_{2}
. We thus obtain an isomorphism

\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cong(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2})^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2}),

where the orthogonal complement is taken in
\mathrm{K}\cong H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
.

By Proposition
2.28
, both
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
descend to the same primitive isotropic element
\overline{\tau}
of
\mathrm{M}\cong{F}^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
. Topologically,
\overline{\tau}
is induced by the class of the torus obtained by parallel transporting
a
around the loop
\gamma
. Let
\tau
be any lift of
\overline{\tau}
to
F^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
. Noting that
\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau
is a rank
2
isotropic sublattice of
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
(and that this sublattice is independent of the choice of lift
\tau
), we obtain an isomorphism

\NS(\mathrm{M})\cong(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau),

where the orthogonal complement is taken in
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
.

Finally, by Lemma
2.29
, we see that the kernel of the map
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\to\NS(\mathrm{M})
is generated by
\zeta
, where the sublattice
\mathbb{Z}\zeta\subset\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
contains the nonzero element
(-K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{2}})=(r_{1}(b),-r_{2}(b))
. Putting things together, we thus obtain the following result.

Proposition 4.20
.

In the setting above, the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\zeta\longrightarrow\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\longrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})\longrightarrow 0

from Proposition
2.28
is realised as

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\zeta\longrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2})^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2})\longrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)\longrightarrow 0,

where the orthogonal complement in the middle term is taken in
\mathrm{K}\cong H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
and the orthogonal complement in the last term is taken in
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
. The symmetric bilinear forms on the last two terms are induced by the bilinear form on
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})
given by Definition
4.16
and the cup product on
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, respectively.

4.4.
Lattice polarisations

Let
\check{L}
be a nondegenerate primitive sublattice of the K3 lattice
\Lambda_{\text{K3}}\cong H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
of signature
(1,\rank(\check{L})-1)
. Let
Y
be a K3 surface which admits an
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation
\check{L}\subset\NS(Y)
(in the sense of Definition
3.19
) and an elliptic fibration
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Assume that
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an allowable loop; then we may define a class
\overline{\tau}\subset F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
, as in the previous section.

Definition 4.21
.

The
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is
compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
if
\check{L}\subset\NS(Y)
contains the class
F
of a fibre of
\pi
, and the orthogonal complement
\check{L}^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
contains the class
\tau
of a lift of
\overline{\tau}
.

Remark 4.22
.

As
\check{L}
is non-degenerate and
F\in\check{L}
, if a lift
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
of
\overline{\tau}
exists, then it is unique, as any other lift
\tau+nF
, for some
n\neq 0
, will not be orthogonal to
\check{L}
.

We will henceforth assume that the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation on
Y
is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
, and we will let
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
denote the class from Definition
4.21
. Let
\Gamma
denote the negative definite lattice
\Gamma:=F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}/\mathbb{Z}F
, where
F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
F
taken in
\check{L}
.

Lemma 4.23
.

There is an embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
.

Proof.

By the compatibility condition, we have
F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}\subset(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}
. As
\check{L}
is nondegenerate and
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
, we cannot have
\tau\in F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}
, so
F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}\cap(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)=\mathbb{Z}F
. It follows that
\Gamma=F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}/\mathbb{Z}F
embeds into
(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
.
∎

By Proposition
4.20
, we obtain an embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow\NS(\mathrm{M})\cong(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
. For the remainder of this section, we will assume that this embedding is primitive; some conditions to ensure this are explored in Section
4.5
. Under this primitivity assumption,
\Gamma\subset\NS(\mathrm{M})
defines a lattice polarisation, in the sense of Definition
2.30
(1).

Let
\hat{\Gamma}
denote the pull-back of
\Gamma
to
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi\cong(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2})^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}\tau_{1}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau_{2})
. Then
\hat{\Gamma}
defines a lifted polarisation on
\Psi^{\perp}_{\mathrm{K}}/\Psi
, in the sense of Definition
2.30
(2). Finally, let
\Gamma_{i}:=\hat{\Gamma}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
denote the intersection polarisation.

Next we define the notion of a lattice polarisation on an elliptic fibration over a disc. This definition is motivated by the definition of a lattice polarisation on a rational elliptic surface, defined in
[
DT18
, Section 3.1]
; the relationship between the two definitions will be explored in Section
4.6
.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
W\to\Delta
be an elliptic fibration over a closed disc with fibre
F_{p}
over a point
p\in\partial\Delta
, such that
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism with right adjoint
r
. Assume that the Euler number
e(W)<12
. Let
N
be a negative definite lattice. An
N
-polarisation on
W
is a primitive embedding
j\colon N\hookrightarrow r(a)^{\perp}/r(a)=\NS(H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z}))
such that

•

q(j(v),[r(b)])=0
for all
v\in N
, where the square brackets
[-]
denote the class in
r(a)^{\perp}/r(a)
, and

•

there exists a set of positive roots
\Delta(N)^{+}\subset N
such that for every
\delta\in\Delta(N)^{+}
, we may write
j(\delta)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}[C_{i}]
, where
n_{i}\geq 0
and
[C_{i}]
are classes of irreducible components of singular fibres in
W
.

The following result shows that this is compatible with our other definitions.

Theorem 4.25
.

With notation as above, assume that
Y_{1}
satisfies
e(Y_{1})<12
. Then the intersection polarisation
\Gamma_{1}:=\hat{\Gamma}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})
defines a
\Gamma_{1}
-polarisation on
Y_{1}
, in the sense of Definition
4.24
.

Proof.

Begin by noting that, by Proposition
2.14
,
q(K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}},K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}})=12-e(Y_{1})>0
. Applying Lemma
2.33
, we see that the map
\hat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
is injective on
\Gamma_{1}
and realises
\Gamma_{1}
as a sublattice of
\Gamma
. As
\Gamma
is negative definite, so is
\Gamma_{1}
.

The condition that
q(j(v),[r_{1}(b)])=0
for all
v\in\Gamma_{1}
follows from Lemma
2.32
and the fact that, by Proposition
2.14
, we have
K_{\mathrm{G}_{1}}=-[r_{1}(b)]
.

To show the second condition from Definition
4.24
, it suffices to show that if
\delta\in\Gamma_{1}
is a class of self-intersection
(-2)
, then either
\delta
or
(-\delta)
is a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres. Let
\delta\in\Gamma_{1}
be any such class. Let
\delta^{\prime}
denote the image of
\delta
under the injective map
\Gamma_{1}\to\Gamma=F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}/\mathbb{Z}F
and let
\overline{\delta}
denote a lift of
\delta^{\prime}
to
F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}\subset H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
. Then
\overline{\delta}
is a
(-2)
-class in
\NS(Y)
and so, replacing
\delta
with
(-\delta)
if necessary, we may assume that
\overline{\delta}
is the class of an effective divisor
[
BHPvdV04
, Proposition VIII.3.7]
. Moreover, as
\overline{\delta}
is orthogonal to the class
F
of a fibre,
\overline{\delta}
must be a nonnegative sum of classes supported on fibres of
\pi
. As
\overline{\delta}
is defined modulo
\mathbb{Z}F
, by subtracting copies of
F
we may assume that
\overline{\delta}
is a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres of
Y
.

Let
\delta_{i}
denote the part of
\overline{\delta}
supported on fibres over points in
\Delta_{i}
. Then each
\delta_{i}
is a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres of
Y_{i}
, and
(\delta,0)
is congruent modulo
\zeta
to
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{1})\oplus\NS(\mathrm{G}_{2})
. Writing
(r_{1}(b),-r_{2}(b))=k\zeta
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, we therefore have
k(\delta_{1}-\delta,\,\delta_{2})=m(r_{1}(b),-r_{2}(b))

for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Applying
\phi_{2}
to the second factor and noting that
\phi_{2}(\delta_{2})=0
(as
\delta_{2}
is a sum of components of singular fibres), we obtain

0=-m\phi_{2}(r_{2}(b))=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{E}}-T_{\phi_{2}})(-mb)=m(e(Y_{2})-12)b

as
\phi_{2}
satisfies Assumption
4.9
. But as
e(Y_{2})-12=12-e(Y_{1})>0
, we must have
m=0
. Therefore
\delta_{2}=0
as well, and so
\delta=\delta_{1}
. We therefore see that
\delta
can be written as a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres of
Y_{1}
.
∎

Remark 4.26
.

The condition that
e(Y_{1})<12
holds if and only if
\deg(\mathrm{G})>0
. As our labelling convention ensures that
e(Y_{1})\leq 12
and we know that
e(Y_{1})+e(Y_{2})=24
, we can never have
e(Y_{2})<12
.

Finally, if
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
has a section, then there is a particularly nice interpretation of the coupling group
Q(\Gamma)
(Definition
2.35
) in this setting.

Proposition 4.27
.

Assume that
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
admits a section
\sigma
and that
\check{L}=\NS(Y)
. Then there is a surjective map
\MW(Y)\to Q(\Gamma)
.

Proof.

Let
H\subset\check{L}
be the copy of the hyperbolic plane lattice spanned by
[\sigma]
and
F
. Then by
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
, we have an orthogonal direct sum decomposition
\check{L}=\NS(Y)=H\oplus\Gamma
. Standard results on elliptic fibrations with section
[
Mir89
, (VII.2.5)]
show that there is an exact sequence

(6)

0\longrightarrow R\longrightarrow\Gamma\longrightarrow\MW(Y)\longrightarrow 0,

where
R\subset\NS(Y)
is the sublattice generated by classes of components of fibres which do not meet
\sigma
.

Let
\Gamma_{i}:=\hat{\Gamma}\cap\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
be the intersection polarisation for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. By Lemma
2.33
we have a map
\varphi\colon\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}\to\Gamma
. We claim that
R
is a sublattice of
\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
. Indeed,
R
is generated by classes of components of fibres which do not meet
\sigma
. Let
C
be any such component. Then
C\subset Y_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and it follows from the description of the Néron-Severi group in Section
4.3
that the class
[C]
of
C
is in
\NS(\mathrm{G}_{i})
. So
[C]\in\Gamma_{i}
. As this holds for all generators of
R
, we see that
R\subset\Gamma_{1}\oplus\Gamma_{2}
. The result then follows by a straightforward diagram chase using sequences (
3
) and (
6
).
∎

4.5.
Primitivity

In this section we explore conditions under which the embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
from Lemma
4.23
is primitive. Throughout we will assume that
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an elliptically fibred K3 surface,
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an allowable loop, and that
Y
admits an
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation that is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
.

Proposition 4.28
.

Suppose that the class
\tau
is
1
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
(Definition
3.30
). Then the embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
from Lemma
4.23
is primitive.

Proof.

By
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
,
1
-admissibility of
\tau
implies that we may decompose
\check{L}^{\perp}
as a direct sum of
H
and another lattice, with
\tau\in H
. By
[
Nik80
, Proposition 1.6.1]
, up to isometry there is a unique primitive embedding
H\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\text{K3}}
, so we may decompose
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}=H\oplus(H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8})
, with
\tau
embedding into the first factor
H
and
\check{L}
embedding into the second factor
H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
.

It then suffices to show that the induced embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
is primitive, where the orthogonal complement of
F
is taken in
H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. But this follows from the fact that
F\in\check{L}
. Indeed, suppose that
\Gamma
is not primitive. Then there exists
\alpha\in F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
and
m>1
such that
\alpha\notin\Gamma
but
m\alpha\in\Gamma
. Let
\hat{\alpha}\in F^{\perp}
be any lift of
\alpha
. Then
\hat{\alpha}\notin\check{L}
, but
m\hat{\alpha}+nF\in\check{L}
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. But as
F\in\check{L}
, this implies that
\hat{\alpha}\notin\check{L}
and
m\hat{\alpha}\in\check{L}
, contradicting primitivity of
\check{L}
.
∎

It is interesting to study the analogue of the doubly admissible condition (Definition
3.31
) in this setting.

Definition 4.29
.

An
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation on
Y
that is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
is called
doubly admissible
if
\tau
is
1
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
and
F
is
1
-admissible in
\check{L}
.

The following result gives some conditions for a quasipolarisation to be doubly admissible.

Proposition 4.30
.

Suppose that
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an elliptically fibred K3 surface with an
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation that is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
. Then:

(1)

If the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is doubly admissible, then
\pi
admits a section.

(2)

If
\tau
is
1
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
and
\pi
admits a section
\sigma
with class
[\sigma]\in\check{L}
, then the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is doubly admissible.

Proof.

To prove (1), by
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
, if the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is doubly admissible then there exists an embedding
H\hookrightarrow\check{L}\subset\NS(Y)
. By
[
Huy16
, Remark 11.1.4]
, this implies that
\pi
admits a section. (2) follows by taking
g
to be the class
g=[\sigma]+F\in\check{L}
in the definition of
1
-admissible (Definition
3.30
).
∎

Remark 4.31
.

Note that in part (1) of the above proposition, the class of the section of
\pi
is not necessarily contained in
\check{L}
.

The next lemma is the analogue of Proposition
3.33
in this setting; it will be useful when we come to discuss mirror symmetry in Section
5
.

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
\check{L}\subset\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
be a nondegenerate primitive sublattice. Let
F
be
1
-admissible in
\check{L}
and
\tau
be
1
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
.
Then
\check{L}
admits an orthogonal decomposition
\check{L}=H\oplus\Gamma
, with
F
primitive in
H
. Moreover, the primitive embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)^{\perp}
factors through primitive embeddings
\Gamma\hookrightarrow H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)^{\perp}
, so that the composition
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)^{\perp}\to(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)
of this primitive embedding with the quotient map is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Applying
[
Dol96
, Lemma 5.4]
to
F
and
\tau
, we obtain orthogonal direct sum decompositions
\check{L}=H\oplus\Gamma
and
\check{L}^{\perp}=H\oplus\Gamma^{\prime}
, for some lattice
\Gamma^{\prime}
, where the two copies of
H
contain
F
and
\tau
respectively. The direct sum
\check{L}\oplus\check{L}^{\perp}
embeds into the K3 lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
and this embedding is primitive on each of the factors, so we obtain a primitive embedding
H\oplus H\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
. By
[
Nik80
, Proposition 1.6.1]
, this embedding is unique up to isometry and, consequently, its orthogonal complement is isometric to
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. As we have a primitive embedding
\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F\hookrightarrow H\oplus H
, we obtain primitive embeddings

\Gamma\hookrightarrow(H\oplus H)^{\perp}_{\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}}\cong H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\tau\oplus\mathbb{Z}F)^{\perp}

and the final map descends to an isomorphism on the quotient.
∎

4.6.
Relationship with lattice polarised rational elliptic surfaces

In this section we will show that the theory above is compatible with the notion of lattice polarisation on a rational elliptic surface introduced in
[
DT18
]
.

We begin by recalling the setup from
[
DT18
, Section 3]
. For consistency with the notation in
[
DT18
]
,
in this section only
we will let
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a rational elliptic surface, such that the fibre
D=\pi^{-1}(\infty)
is of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{d}
for some
1\leq d\leq 9
. Write
D=\bigcup_{i=1}^{d}D_{i}
as a union of irreducible components, numbered cyclically. We assume that
\pi
does not have multiple fibres, which for rational elliptic surfaces is equivalent to the existence of a section.

Let
U
denote the open set
U:=Y\setminus D
. Let
{\Delta}
denote the closed set
\Delta:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus B_{\varepsilon}(\infty)
, where
B_{\varepsilon}(\infty)\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an open disc of radius
\varepsilon>0
centred at
\infty
and
\varepsilon
is chosen small enough that the only singular fibre over the closure
\overline{B_{\varepsilon}(\infty)}
is
D
, and let
W
denote the closed set
W:=\pi^{-1}(\Delta)
. Note that
W
is a closed manifold with boundary, so there is an intersection pairing on
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
, and that
W
is a deformation retract of
U
. Finally, let
p\in\partial\Delta
be a point and let
F=\pi^{-1}(p)
be the corresponding smooth fibre.

Under this setup, we note that the Euler number
e(W)=e(Y)-d=12-d
and that the action of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
is given by the inverse of the action of anticlockwise monodromy around an
\mathrm{I}_{d}
fibre. Thus, from Kodaira’s classification, there exists a symplectic basis
(a,b)
for
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
such that the action of anticlockwise monodromy around
\partial\Delta
is given by

\begin{pmatrix}1&d\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-d\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&e(W)-12\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Let
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
denote the asymptotic charge map and
r
denote its right adjoint.

Proposition 4.33
.

With notation as above,
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism.

Proof.

If we can prove that
r(a)\in H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
is primitive, then Assumption
4.9
will hold in this setting and so, by Theorem
4.10
,
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
will be a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism.

Let
\Delta^{\prime}:=B_{\delta}(\infty)
be an open disc of radius
\delta>\varepsilon
centred at
\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where
\delta
is chosen small enough that the only singular fibre over
{B_{\delta}(\infty)}
is
D
, and let
W^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(\Delta^{\prime})
. Then
Y=W\cup W^{\prime}
and Mayer-Vietoris gives

H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{1}(W\cap W^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{1}(W;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{1}(W^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow 0.

Now,
W\cap W^{\prime}
deformation retracts onto
\pi^{-1}(\partial\Delta)
, which is a torus bundle over
S^{1}
with monodromy
\begin{pmatrix}1&-d\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
. It is then easy to compute that
H_{1}(W\cap W^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}s\oplus\mathbb{Z}b\oplus(\mathbb{Z}/d\mathbb{Z})a
, where
s
is the image of the section of
Y
under the map
H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(W\cap W^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
and
(a,b)
are the classes from
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
pushed-forward by the inclusion
F\hookrightarrow W\cap W^{\prime}
.

Moreover,
W^{\prime}
deformation retracts onto
D
, which is an
\mathrm{I}_{d}
fibre with
d\geq 1
, so
H_{1}(W^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}b
(the class
a
is the vanishing cycle). Thus, by the Mayer-Vietoris sequence above, we see that
H_{1}(W;\mathbb{Z})
must be a torsion group generated by the push-forward of
a
under the inclusion
F\hookrightarrow W
.

From the long exact sequence of a pair we have

H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{1}(W;\mathbb{Z}),

where
f
is the push-forward under the inclusion. By the argument above,
H_{1}(W;\mathbb{Z})
is generated by
f(a)
, so
f(b)=0
. Thus
b\in\im(\phi)
. Choose any
v\in H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
with
\phi(v)=b
. Then
\langle v,r(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=\langle b,a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=1,

by adjunction. As the Seifert pairing is integral, it follows that
r(a)
must be primitive.
∎

Based on
[
DT18
, Section 3.1]
we define a lattice polarisation on
Y
as follows.

Definition 4.34
.

Let
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a rational elliptic surface with section, let
D\subset Y
be a fibre of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{d}
, and let
[F]\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong\Pic(Y)
be the class of a fibre of
\pi
. Let
N
be a negative definite lattice. An
N
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
is a primitive embedding
j\colon N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
such that

•

j(\beta).D_{i}=0
for all
\beta\in N
and all irreducible components
D_{i}\subset D
(where
1\leq i\leq d
), and

•

there exists a set of positive roots
\Delta(N)^{+}\subset N
such that every
\delta\in j(\Delta(N)^{+})
is the class of an effective divisor from
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 4.35
.

This definition differs from
[
DT18
, Definition 3.6]
in that we embed
N
into
[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
, rather than into
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong\Pic(Y)
. These definitions are not equivalent: an embedding
N\hookrightarrow H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
satisfying
[
DT18
, Definition 3.6]
induces an embedding
N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
as above, but this latter embedding is not necessarily primitive. Conversely, an embedding
N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
as above does not uniquely determine an embedding
N\hookrightarrow H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
satisfying
[
DT18
, Definition 3.6]
. However, it follows from the results of
[
DT18
, Section 3]
that there is still a well-defined period mapping and corresponding moduli theory for lattice polarised rational elliptic surfaces in the sense of Definition
4.34
.

The aim of this section is to show that Definition
4.34
is compatible with Definition
4.24
. We begin with a pair of lemmas that relate the lattices that we are interested in, before proving the main result (Theorem
4.38
).

Lemma 4.36
.

There is a primitive lattice embedding

H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}[F]\hookrightarrow r(a)^{\perp}\subset H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z}),

where
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
is equipped with the bilinear form induced by the topological intersection pairing,
[F]
is the totally degenerate class of the fibre
F
in
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
, and
r(a)^{\perp}\subset H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
is equipped with the negative of the Seifert pairing (which is symmetric on
r(a)^{\perp}
as
r(a)
is point-like).

Moreover, the totally degenerate class
r(a)\subset r(a)^{\perp}
is in the image of this embedding, so we have a primitive lattice embedding

H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Z}r(a)\oplus\mathbb{Z}[F])\hookrightarrow r(a)^{\perp}/r(a),

where
r^{\perp}(a)/r(a)
is the Néron-Severi lattice of
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
. The image of this embedding is those classes
v
in the Néron-Severi lattice with
q(v,[r(b)])=0
.

Proof.

From the long exact sequence of a pair we have

H_{2}(F;\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\longrightarrow}}H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z}),

and the map
f
takes the topological intersection product on
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
to the negative of the Seifert pairing on
H_{2}(W,F;\mathbb{Z})
. The image of the first map is
\mathbb{Z}[F]
. To prove the first part of the lemma, it thus suffices to show that the image of the map
f
is a primitive sublattice of
r(a)^{\perp}
. Let
v\in\im(f)
be any class. Then
\langle v,r(a)\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=\langle\phi(v),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
, as
\phi(v)=0
by exactness, so
\im(f)\subset r(a)^{\perp}
. Primitivity follows from the fact that
H_{1}(F;\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free.

To prove the second part, by exactness
\im(f)=\ker(\phi)
. As the map
\phi
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism, by Lemma
2.19
we have
\phi\circ r(a)=0
and thus
r(a)\in\ker(\phi)=\im(f)
. Finally, note that
v\in\im(f)=\ker(\phi)
if and only if
\langle\phi(v),a\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=\langle\phi(v),b\rangle_{\mathrm{E}}=0
. A class
v\in r(a)^{\perp}
satisfies this if and only if
\langle v,r(b)\rangle_{\mathrm{Sft}}=-q(v,[r(b)])=0
.
∎

Lemma 4.37
.

There is a primitive lattice embedding

H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}r(a)\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}\subset H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong\Pic(Y),

where
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
is equipped with the bilinear form induced by the topological intersection pairing and
[F]
is the class of a fibre
F
.

Moreover, the totally degenerate class
[F]
is in the image of this embedding, so we have a primitive lattice embedding

H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Z}r(a)\oplus\mathbb{Z}[F])\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F].

The image of this embedding is those classes
\beta
with
\beta.D_{i}=0
for all irreducible components
D_{i}\subset D
(where
1\leq i\leq d
).

Proof.

Note first that, under Poincaré duality, we have an isomorphism of lattices
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
, where
H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
is equipped with the topological intersection pairing
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}
.

Set
D_{0}:=D_{1}\cap D_{d}
; note that
D_{0}
is a point. Define
U_{0}=Y
and
U_{i+1}=U_{i}\setminus D_{i}
for each
0\leq i\leq d
; note that
U_{d+1}=U
. Then the embeddings
U_{i+1}\hookrightarrow U_{i}
induce pushforward maps
H_{2}(U_{i+1};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(U_{i};\mathbb{Z})
, which fit into the following long exact sequence that is Poincaré dual to the exact sequence of cohomology with compact support

H_{1}(U_{i}\cap D_{i};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(U_{i+1};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{2}(U_{i};\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow H_{0}(U_{i}\cap D_{i};\mathbb{Z}).

Noting that
U_{i}\cap D_{i}
deformation retracts onto a point if
0\leq i\leq d-1
and a loop
S^{1}
homotopic to the vanishing cycle
a
if
i=d
, we see that the map
H_{2}(U_{i+1};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(U_{i};\mathbb{Z})
is injective if
0\leq i\leq d-1
and has kernel
\mathbb{Z}\alpha
generated by the class
\alpha
of a torus obtained by parallel transporting
a
around
\partial\Delta
if
i=d
. Moreover, the image of this map consists of those classes
\beta\in H_{2}(U_{i};\mathbb{Z})
with
\langle\beta,D_{i}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=0
.

Composing these maps for
0\leq i\leq d
, we see that the map in homology
H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
induced by the inclusion
U\to Y
has kernel
\mathbb{Z}\alpha
and image consisting of those classes
\beta\in H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
with
\langle\beta,D_{i}\rangle_{\mathrm{top}}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq d
; in particular the image is contained in
[F]^{\perp}
. Moreover, by deformation retraction we have an isomorphism
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{2}(U;\mathbb{Z})
, which identifies
\alpha
with
-r(a)
(see Section
4.3
).

Putting everything together, we therefore obtain an injective map of lattices
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}r(a)\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}
, proving the first part of the lemma. The image of this map consists of those classes
\beta\in[F]^{\perp}
with
\beta.D_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq d
. As the class
[F]
satisfies
[F].D_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq d
, this class is in the image, and the second statement follows.
∎

Theorem 4.38
.

Let
N
be a negative definite lattice. With notation as above, if
j\colon N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
is an
N
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
, in the sense of Definition
4.34
, then
j
induces an
N
-polarisation on
W
, in the sense of Definition
4.24
.

Conversely, if
j^{\prime}\colon N\hookrightarrow r^{\perp}(a)/r(a)
is an
N
-polarisation on
W
, in the sense of Definition
4.24
, then
j^{\prime}
induces an
N
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
, in the sense of Definition
4.34
.

Proof.

Note first that, by Lemmas
4.36
and
4.37
, we have embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r(a)^{\perp}/r(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Z}r(a)\oplus\mathbb{Z}[F])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[F]}^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}{[F]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

By Lemma
4.37
, if
j\colon N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
is an
N
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
, then
j(N)
is in the image of the embedding
\psi
, so there is a primitive embedding
N\hookrightarrow H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Z}r(a)\oplus\mathbb{Z}[F])
. Composing with the primitive embedding
\varphi
, we obtain an embedding
N\hookrightarrow r(a)^{\perp}/r(a)
, such that
q(v,[r(b)])=0
for all
v\in N
.

If
\delta\in\Delta(N)^{+}
, then since
j(\delta).D_{i}=0
for all components
D_{i}\subset D
and
j(\delta)
is the class of an effective divisor,
j(\delta)
can be represented as an effective sum of classes of components of fibres in
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
. Quotienting by
\mathbb{Z}[F]
, we see that
j(\delta)
can be expressed as a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres of
W
. We thus obtain an
N
-polarisation on
W
, in the sense of Definition
4.24
.

Conversely, by Lemma
4.36
, if
j\colon N\hookrightarrow r(a)^{\perp}/r(a)
is an
N
-polarisation on
W
, then
j(N)
is in the image of the embedding
\varphi
, so there is a primitive embedding
N\hookrightarrow H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Z}r(a)\oplus\mathbb{Z}[F])
. Composing with the primitive embedding
\psi
, we obtain an embedding
N\hookrightarrow[F]^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}[F]
, such that
v.D_{i}=0
for all
v\in N
and all
1\leq i\leq d
.

If
\delta\in\Delta(N)^{+}
, then
j(\delta)
is a nonnegative sum of classes of components of singular fibres in
H_{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
. But every such class is also the class of an effective divisor from
H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
. We thus obtain an
N
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
, in the sense of Definition
4.34
.
∎

5.
Mirror Symmetry

The DHT philosophy, introduced in
[
DHT17
]
, postulates a mirror correspondence between Tyurin degenerations and codimension
1
fibrations on Calabi-Yau manifolds. The aim of this section is to make this correspondence precise in the case of K3 surfaces.

5.1.
Mirror Pairs

We begin by defining when a Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces and an elliptically fibred K3 surface form a
mirror pair
.

Let
L
and
\check{L}
be two nondegenerate sublattices of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
of signatures
(1,\rank(L)-1)
and
(1,\rank(\check{L})-1)
, respectively. Note that we do not, a priori, assume any relationship between
L
and
\check{L}
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, with general fibre
X
and central fibre
X_{0}=V_{1}\cup_{C}V_{2}
. Then we have an embedding
L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}/{I}
, by Lemma
3.22
. Moreover, by Lemma
2.33
and Proposition
3.17
there is a map

\varphi_{X}\colon K_{V_{1}}^{\perp}\oplus K_{V_{2}}^{\perp}\longrightarrow I^{\perp}/I

which is injective on each of the factors
K_{V_{i}}^{\perp}\subset\NS(V_{i})
.

Let
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
be an elliptically fibred K3 surface which admits an
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation. Assume that
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an allowable loop, such that the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
. Let
p\in\gamma
be a point, let
F_{p}=\pi^{-1}(p)
be the fibre over
p
, and let
F
denote the class of
F_{p}
in
\check{L}
. If
\Gamma:=F^{\perp}_{\check{L}}/\mathbb{Z}F
, then by Lemma
4.23
there is an embedding
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
, where
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
is the class from Definition
4.21
.
Then
\gamma
induces a splitting
\mathbb{P}^{1}=\Delta_{1}\cup_{\gamma}\Delta_{2}
of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
into a pair of discs; let
\pi_{i}\colon Y_{i}\to\Delta_{i}
denote the induced elliptic fibrations over discs. By Lemma
2.33
and Proposition
4.20
there is a map

\varphi_{Y}\colon[r_{1}(b)]^{\perp}\oplus[r_{2}(b)]^{\perp}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)

which is injective on each of the factors
[r_{i}(b)]^{\perp}\subset\NS(H_{2}(Y_{i},F_{p};\mathbb{Z}))
.

Definition 5.1
.

A Tyurin degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and elliptically fibred K3 surface
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
as above are called a
mirror pair
if the following conditions hold.

(1)

The embeddings
L\hookrightarrow I^{\perp}/{I}
and
\Gamma\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
are primitive.

(2)

There is an isometry
\psi\colon(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}I^{\perp}/I
satisfying

\psi\circ\varphi_{Y}([r_{i}(b)]^{\perp})=\varphi_{X}(K_{V_{i}}^{\perp}),

for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.

(3)

\psi
can be lifted to an isometry
\hat{\psi}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\to H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
taking
\tau
and
F
to primitive generators
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
of
I
, respectively.

(4)

For some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, either

(a)

\tau
is
m
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
and
\hat{\psi}(\check{L}^{\perp})=H(m)\oplus L
, with
e_{1}=\hat{\psi}(\tau)\in H(m)
, or

(b)

e_{1}
is
m
-admissible in
L^{\perp}
and
\hat{\psi}^{-1}(L^{\perp})=H(m)\oplus\check{L}
, with
\tau=\hat{\psi}^{-1}(e_{1})\in H(m)
.

Remark 5.2
.

By Theorem
2.18
and Propositions
2.21
and
2.37
, an isometry satisfying condition (2) always exists if the corresponding pseudolattices
\mathrm{G}
on both sides have the same degree. In all cases except degrees
8
and
8^{\prime}
this reduces to checking that
12-e(Y_{1})=K_{V_{1}}^{2}
.

Using conditions (1), (3) and (4) from Definition
5.1
, it is straightforward to show that
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
, where the orthogonal complement is taken in
I^{\perp}/I
. Our next result shows that if our polarisations are doubly admissible, then the converse also holds. This allows for a dramatic simplification of Definition
5.1
in the doubly admissible setting.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that the
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation on
Y
are both doubly admissible, in the sense of Definitions
3.31
and
4.29
. Then
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are a mirror pair, in the sense of Definition
5.1
, if and only if there is an isometry
\psi\colon(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}I^{\perp}/I
satisfying
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
and
\psi\circ\varphi_{Y}([r_{i}(b)]^{\perp})=\varphi_{X}(K_{V_{i}}^{\perp})
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

The forward direction is immediate from the discussion above; it suffices to prove the converse. So assume that there is an isometry
\psi\colon(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}I^{\perp}/I
satisfying
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
and
\psi\circ\varphi_{Y}([r_{i}(b)]^{\perp})=\varphi_{X}(K_{V_{i}}^{\perp})
, for each
i\in\{1,2\}
; we check the four conditions for
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
to be a mirror pair from Definition
5.1
.

By Propositions
3.33
and
4.28
, condition (1) is a consequence of the doubly admissible assumption. Moreover, condition (2) holds by assumption.

By the proof of Lemma
3.32
, there is an isometry
\phi_{X}\colon H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})\to H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
, taking primitive generators
e_{1},e_{2}\in I
to primitive isotropic vectors in each of the first two factors of
H
and taking
L
to a primitive sublattice of the remaining
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. Similarly, by the proof of Lemma
4.32
, there is an isometry
\phi_{Y}\colon H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\to H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
, taking
\tau
and
F
to primitive isotropic vectors in each of the first two factors of
H
and taking
\Gamma
to a primitive sublattice of the remaining
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
. As primitive isotropic vectors in
H
are unique up to isometry, we may arrange that
\phi_{Y}(\tau)=\phi_{X}(e_{1})
and
\phi_{Y}(F)=\phi_{X}(e_{2})
. Moreover, by Proposition
3.33
and Lemma
4.32
, there are isomorphisms
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\cong I^{\perp}/I
and
H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}\cong(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)^{\perp}/(\mathbb{Z}F\oplus\mathbb{Z}\tau)
compatible with the quotients; consequently the isometry
\psi
from condition (2) may be lifted to an isometry
\overline{\psi}
of
H\oplus H\oplus H\oplus E_{8}\oplus E_{8}
which fixes the first two factors of
H
. Then
\phi_{X}^{-1}\circ\overline{\psi}\circ\phi_{Y}
is the required isometry for condition (3).

Finally, it follows from the definition of doubly admissible that
\tau
is
1
-admissible in
\check{L}^{\perp}
and
e_{1}
is
1
-admissible in
L^{\perp}
. By the construction above and the assumption that
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
, we have
\overline{\psi}\circ\phi_{Y}(\Gamma)=\phi_{X}(L)^{\perp}
. Consequently, we have a finite index sublattice
H\oplus H\oplus\phi_{X}(L)\oplus(\overline{\psi}\circ\phi_{Y}(\Gamma))
of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{K}3}
, where
\phi_{Y}(\tau)=\phi_{X}(e_{1})
and
\phi_{Y}(F)=\phi_{X}(e_{2})
are primitive isotropic elements in each of the two factors of
H
; both parts (a) and (b) of condition (4) follow.
∎

5.2.
Compatibility with other forms of mirror symmetry

In this section we explore the compatibility between the notion of mirror pairs established in Definition
5.1
and some established notions of mirror symmetry.

5.2.1.
Lattice polarised mirror symmetry for K3 surfaces

Given a mirror pair in the sense of Definition
5.1
, condition (4) in the definition immediately implies that a general fibre
X
of the Tyurin degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and the elliptically fibred K3 surface
Y
are mirror as lattice polarised K3 surfaces, in the sense of
[
Dol96
]
.

Note that if
m=1
in condition (4), then (4a) holds if and only if (4b) does and the mirror correspondence is a duality; see
[
Dol96
, Section 6]
.

5.2.2.
Homological mirror symmetry for quasi del Pezzo surfaces and quasi LG models

In
[
HT20
]
it is shown that if
V
is a quasi del Pezzo surface with smooth anticanonical divisor
C
, then there exists a genus
1
Lefschetz fibration
W\to\Delta
over a disc with a smooth fibre
F_{p}
over a point
p
on the boundary, so that there are isomorphisms of pseudolattices making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
i\colon C\hookrightarrow V
is the inclusion and
\phi
is the asymptotic charge map.

To see how this is a homological mirror symmetry statement, let
\mathbf{F}(F_{p})
denote the derived Fukaya category of
F_{p}
and
\mathbf{F}(W)
denote the derived Fukaya-Seidel category of
Y
. Then there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{F}(F_{p}))\cong H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
which takes the Euler pairing to the intersection form, and an isomorphism
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{F}(W))\cong H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
which takes the Euler pairing to the Seifert pairing
[
Sug16
, Section 6.1]
. Homological mirror symmetry predicts a derived equivalence between
\mathbf{D}(V)
and
\mathbf{F}(W)
, compatible with the derived equivalence between
\mathbf{D}(C)
and
\mathbf{F}(F_{p})
established in
[
PZ98
,
AS10
]
; the statement above can be thought of as a shadow of this on the level of numerical Grothendieck groups.

Our notion of mirror pairs enjoys the following compatibility with the theory above.

Theorem 5.4
.

Given a mirror pair in the sense of Definition
5.1
, for each
j\in\{1,2\}
there are isomorphisms of pseudolattices which make the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(Y_{j},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

Condition (2) in Definition
5.1
and Proposition
2.37
imply that the isometry
\psi
lifts to isometries
\psi_{j}\colon\NS(H_{2}(Y_{j},F_{p};\mathbb{Z}))\to\NS(V_{j})
such that
\psi_{j}([r_{j}(b)])=-K_{V_{j}}
. Existence of the isomorphisms in the theorem then follows from Proposition
2.21
.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The pseudolattice isomorphisms
H_{2}(Y_{j},F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V_{j}))
do not induce a lattice isometry between
H^{2}_{c}(U;\mathbb{Z})/\mathbb{Z}F
and
H^{0}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})\oplus\xi^{\perp}\oplus H^{4}(X_{0};\mathbb{Z})
, as the Chern character is not defined over
\mathbb{Z}
(see Lemma
3.12
). However, they do induce a lattice isometry between
F^{\perp}/\mathbb{Z}F
and
H^{0}_{\lim}(X)\oplus I^{\perp}/I\oplus H^{4}_{\lim}(X)
(see Lemma
3.14
), and (up to sign) this isomorphism induces
\psi
on the Néron-Severi lattices.

5.2.3.
Mirror symmetry for lattice polarised del Pezzo surfaces

In
[
DT18
]
, Doran and the second author conjectured a mirror symmetric relationship between lattice polarised weak del Pezzo surfaces and lattice polarised rational elliptic surfaces. This should be thought of as a lattice polarised enhancement of Auroux’s, Katzarkov’s, and Orlov’s
[
AKO06
]
description of Landau-Ginzburg models of del Pezzo surfaces as open sets inside rational elliptic surfaces. The aim of this section is to explore the relationship between this conjecture and the theory presented here.

In more detail, let
V
be a weak del Pezzo surface with smooth anticanonical divisor
C
, such that
(V,C)
admits an
N
-polarisation by a negative definite lattice
N
, in the sense of Definition
3.27
. Let
\check{N}
denote the orthogonal complement of
N
in
K_{V}^{\perp}
; note that
\check{N}
is also negative definite, as
V
is weak del Pezzo (see Lemma
2.20
). Then, roughly speaking,
[
DT18
, Conjecture 5.1]
postulates that the mirror to an
N
-polarised weak del Pezzo surface
(V,C)
with
C^{2}=d
should be given by the open set
Y\setminus D
in an
\check{N}
-polarised rational elliptic surface
(Y,D)
(in the sense of Definition
4.34
), where
D\subset Y
is a fibre of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{d}
. For a more detailed statement we refer the reader to
[
DT18
]
; in particular, we note that there is some subtlety when
d=8
.

By Proposition
4.33
, a rational elliptic surface
(Y,D)
as above determines a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism
\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
, where
W
is obtained by removing an open tubular neighbourhood around
D
and
F_{p}
is a fibre on the boundary of
W
. Moreover, by Theorem
4.38
, specifying an
\check{N}
-polarisation on
(Y,D)
, in the sense of Definition
4.34
, is equivalent to specifying an
\check{N}
-polarisation on
W
, in the sense of Definition
4.24
. These observations allow us to reformulate
[
DT18
, Conjecture 5.1]
in the language of this paper, as follows.

Conjecture 5.6

(Mirror symmetry for lattice polarised weak del Pezzo surfaces)
.

The mirror to an
N
-polarised weak del Pezzo surface
(V,C)
is given by an elliptic fibration
W\to\Delta
over a closed disc, with fibre
F_{p}
over a point
p\in\partial\Delta
, such that

(1)

\phi\colon H_{2}(W,F_{p};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(F_{p};\mathbb{Z})
is a quasi del Pezzo homomorphism which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(V))\to\mathrm{K}_{0}^{\mathrm{num}}(\mathbf{D}(C))
, and

(2)

W
is
\check{N}
-polarised, in the sense of Definition
4.24
, where
\check{N}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
N
in
K_{V}^{\perp}
.

Now we explore the relationship between this conjecture and the notion of a mirror pair from Definition
5.1
. In particular, if we have a mirror pair with
12-e(Y_{1})=K_{V_{1}}^{2}>0
, so
V_{1}
is weak del Pezzo, we would like to know when the intersection polarisations on
(V_{1},C)
and
Y_{1}
satisfy Conjecture
5.6
.

From Proposition
2.36
, we expect the answer to be related to the coupling group. The following lemma will be helpful.

Lemma 5.7
.

Suppose we have a mirror pair, in the sense of Definition
5.1
, and assume that
12-e(Y_{1})=K_{V_{1}}^{2}>0
. Then the coupling group
Q(L)
is torsion if and only if the coupling group
Q(\Gamma)
is torsion.

Proof.

This is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
2.36
and the fact that, for a mirror pair, we have
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
.
∎

Now we state the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.8
.

Suppose that we have a mirror pair, in the sense of Definition
5.1
, and assume that
V_{1}
is weak del Pezzo. Then
(V_{1},C)
is an
L_{1}
-polarised weak del Pezzo surface (in the sense of Definition
3.27
) and
Y_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
is a
\Gamma_{1}
-polarised fibration over a disc (in the sense of Definition
4.24
), where
L_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1}
denote the intersection polarisations induced by
L
and
\Gamma
respectively.

Moreover, if the coupling groups
Q(L)
and
Q(\Gamma)
are torsion, then
(V_{1},C)
and
Y_{1}\to\Delta_{1}
satisfy Conjecture
5.6
, with
N=L_{1}
and
\check{N}=\Gamma_{1}
.

Remark 5.9
.

By Lemma
5.7
, if one of
Q(L)
and
Q(\Gamma)
is torsion, then both are. By Proposition
4.27
, if
\check{L}=\NS(Y)
then a sufficient condition for this to hold is that
\MW(Y)
is torsion.

Proof.

(V_{1},C)
is an
L_{1}
-polarised weak del Pezzo surface by Theorem
3.28
and
Y_{1}
is a
\Gamma_{1}
-polarised fibration over a disc by Theorem
4.25
(noting that
12-e(Y_{1})=K_{V_{1}}^{2}>0
).

Given this, condition (1) from Conjecture
5.6
follows from Theorem
5.4
. Finally, to prove condition (2) we need to show that the orthogonal complement of
L_{1}
in
{K_{V_{1}}^{\perp}}
is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{1}
; this follows from Proposition
2.36
and the fact that, for a mirror pair, we have
\psi(\Gamma)=L^{\perp}
.
∎

5.3.
The DHT conjecture

The DHT philosophy
[
DHT17
]
postulates that there is a mirror correspondence between Tyurin degenerations and codimension
1
fibrations on Calabi-Yau manifolds. Moreover, it suggests that it should be possible to divide the base of the fibration into pieces which “look like” the Landau-Ginzburg models of the components of the Tyurin degeneration.

The K3 case of this idea is explored somewhat in
[
DHT17
, Section 4]
, which presents some evidence for this idea but does not state a concrete conjecture. Using the theory developed in this paper, we are now able to state a precise version of the DHT conjecture for K3 surfaces.

Conjecture 5.10

(DHT for K3 surfaces)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be an
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces, with general fibre
X
. Assume that there is a primitive
e\in I
which is
m
-admissible in
L^{\perp}
and let
\check{L}=e^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}/\mathbb{Z}e
denote the mirror lattice to
L
. Let
Y
be an
\check{L}
-quasipolarised K3 surface. Then
Y
admits an elliptic fibration
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
and there exists an allowable loop
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
, so that
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are a mirror pair in the sense of Definition
5.1
.

Conversely, let
Y
be an
L^{\prime}
-quasipolarised K3 surface which admits an elliptic fibration
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Suppose that there is an allowable loop
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that the
L^{\prime}
-quasipolarisation is compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
. Assume that the class
\tau
is
m
-admissible in
(L^{\prime})^{\perp}
and let
\check{L}^{\prime}=\tau^{\perp}_{(L^{\prime})^{\perp}}/\mathbb{Z}\tau
denote the mirror lattice to
L^{\prime}
. Then there exists an
\check{L}^{\prime}
-polarised Tyurin degeneration of K3 surfaces
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
, so that
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
and
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are a mirror pair in the sense of Definition
5.1
.

Remark 5.11
.

The difficult part of this conjecture is the choice of the loop
\gamma
or, conversely, the choice of birational model for the Tyurin degeneration. The examples in
[
DPT23
]
show that knowledge of the class
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
is not sufficient to determine
\gamma
and, conversely, knowledge of the the rank
2
isotropic sublattice
I\subset(\check{L}^{\prime})^{\perp}
is not sufficient to determine the birational model for the Tyurin degeneration.

Remark 5.12
.

We expect the conjecture above to relate doubly admissible Tyurin degenerations to doubly admissible elliptically fibred K3 surfaces.

Indeed, suppose that
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a doubly admissible
L
-polarised Tyurin degeneration. Choose primitive generators
e_{1},e_{2}\in I
as in Definition
3.31
. By Proposition
3.33
and the proof of Lemma
3.32
, we may decompose
L=H\oplus H\oplus\Gamma
, where
e_{1}
(resp.
e_{2}
) is a primitive element of the first (resp. second) factor of
H
.

By Proposition
3.38
, the
1
-cusp in the Baily-Borel compactification of the moduli space of
L
-polarised K3 surfaces corresponding to
I\subset L^{\perp}
is incident to a unique
0
-cusp which, up to isometry, we may assume corresponds to the isotropic vector
e_{1}
. Let
\check{L}=(e_{1})^{\perp}_{L^{\perp}}/\mathbb{Z}e_{1}
be the mirror lattice. Then
\check{L}\cong H\oplus\Gamma
and
e_{2}
descends to a primitive isotropic vector in the factor
H\subset\check{L}
. It follows that
e_{2}\in\check{L}
and
e_{1}\in\check{L}^{\perp}
are both
1
-admissible. Moreover, if
Y
is a K3 surface with
\NS(Y)\cong\check{L}
, it follows from
[
Huy16
, Remark 8.2.13]
that
Y
is elliptically fibred and that we may choose the isometry
\check{L}\to\NS(Y)
so that
e_{2}
is taken to the class of a fibre. If Conjecture
5.10
holds, we expect that the class
\tau\in\check{L}^{\perp}
defined by the loop
\gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
should equal
e_{1}
; if this is the case then the
\check{L}
-quasipolarisation on
Y
will be doubly admissible and compatible with
\pi
and
\gamma
.

Conversely, if
\pi\colon Y\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an elliptically fibred K3 surface with a doubly admissible
L^{\prime}
-quasipolarisation, then
F\in L^{\prime}
and
\tau\in(L^{\prime})^{\perp}
are both
1
-admissible. If
\check{L}^{\prime}=\tau^{\perp}_{(L^{\prime})^{\perp}}/\mathbb{Z}\tau
is the mirror lattice to
L^{\prime}
, then
F
and
\tau
give rise to a doubly admissible isotropic sublattice
I\subset(\check{L}^{\prime})^{\perp}
. If Conjecture
5.10
holds, we expect this
I\subset(\check{L}^{\prime})^{\perp}
to be the rank
2
isotropic sublattice corresponding to the mirror
\check{L}^{\prime}
-polarised Tyurin degeneration.
